PRESENTATION TO MR. J. E. SECKER.

Approaching Marriage.

There was an interesting gathering in the Daresbury Sessions Room on Tuesday evening, _when the tenantry of Sir Gilbert Greenall's estate at Walton assembled to present Mr. J. E. Seeker with a token of their esteem on the occasion of his marriage to Miss Gertrude Litton, of the Uplands, Moore, which takes place on Wednesday next.—Mr. Seeker is sub-agent to Sir Gilbert under his uncle, Mr. John White, and has dis- charged his duties in such away as to gain the goodwill of all the tenantry. The present took the form of a handsome and massive solid silver tray bearing the following inscription :—" Presented to John Eustace Seeker, Esq., on the occasion of his marriage, Septem- ber 30th, 1903, as a mark of respect and good feeling, by the tenantry of the Walton estate."

Mr. Henry Mawd.sley presided, and on the platform were also Mr. Seeker, the Rev. F. Clifton Smith, Mr. A. Broome, Mr. John Whitlow, and Mr. Charles W. Phillips. Mr. Beswick, who was born at Walton, and is now in his 70th year, made the presentation in a few suitable remarks. He said the tray was only a small token of the respect in which Mr. Seeker was held by all the tenantry. He wished him much happiness, and hoped he would live long to look upon that gift. (Applause.) Mr. Secker,in reply said he was not going to lock the tray up, as was sometimes done in such cases,in the hope that it ,would be handed down to one's grand-children or., some distant generation, but he was going to make daily use of it so as it would always be before him to remind him of them. It was 14 years since he first knew the estate, when he attended the audit at the Red Lion, Preston Brook. At first they met twice a year, but as years went on he got to know them better and to see them oftener, and the fact remained that he had never yet found he had not been welcomed at a house on the estate for whatever pnrposelbe had gone. He felt most grateful for the special kindness shown to him during the last six months. When in March aparticular friend whom he missed very much, lay upon his bed of sickness, and the agent of their estate was away from home seriously ill also, Sir Gilbert Greenall called upon him (Mr. Seeker) to act forthe time being as agent, sub- agent, bailiff' and general help all round. For the consideration shown to him at that time he was very grateful. It was an experience that many would sell their last clothes to obtain, and it was an experience that some would doubtless shrink from. It meant, no doubt, a great deal of labour, bat it was lessened con- siderably by the way he was received. He hoped at some future date the same kindly affectionate regard between the tenants and the agent, if he were permitted to see that day, would exist as had always been thecase for the past 30 or 40 years. He thanked them immensely for their valuable and beautiful present i which would always be useful and acceptable, and for the kind words spoken. (Applause). The Rev, F. Clifton Smith said he spoke from the bottom of his heart when he said that the Walton ten- antry could not possibly have two men so absolutely capable of fulfilling those great duties they had to do Mr. John White and Mr. Seeker. (Applause). Mr. Seeker and his intended bride had their heartiest wishes and he hoped that their marriage would be a happy one, and that they might have many cups of tea off that beautiful tray. (Applause). Mr. Broome and Mr. Whitlow also made some appro- priate remarks wishing Mr. Seeker and Miss Litton every happiness.'

===== 0002601_19030926_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
--et-- —_ CHARGE AGAINST AN EX-SOLDIER. Court-Martial at Orford Barracks.

A district court-martial was held at Orford Barracks on Tuesday on William T. Dickinson formerly colour- sergeant and orderly-room clerk at the depot of the King's Liverpool Regiment, Orford Barracks, who carries on business as a newsagent in Scotland- road, Warrington. He was charged on three counts with embezzling public money in sums of £l, £2, and .£3. On the advice of his solicitor, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, he pleaded guilty. The summary of evidence was put in and read, from which it appeared that the money had been sent by soldiers for their discharge, and had been appropriated by the prisoner. Mr. Jenkins addressed the court in mitigation of punishment, and pointed out that prisoner had eleven years' service, and he had also been to the front. When confronted with the fact that the money was missing he candidly admitted that he was the wrong-doer, and so saved suspicion falling upon gentlemen who might have suffered much in consequence. His manly conduct in that respect might be fairly taken into account in measuring out his punishment. Again, when he went on to the Reserve he made no attempti to leave the dis- trict, but set up in asmall business n Warrington, and traded with the Barracks. At the present moment there was due to him by trade at the barracks £8 10s. 3d. Having regard to the circumstances surrounding the case, he submitted that the justice of it might be fairly met by allowing the prisoner to refund, out of the £8 10s. 3d., the amount he admitted taking. The prisoner had been already before the sivil authorities and re- manded, and publicity given to the charges. He was practically ruined in his business and had a wife and child depending upon him. Two officers (Captain Eldridge and Sergeant-Major Tarrant) testified to Dickinson's previous good conduct. Captain Osbomeread a statement, which showed that prisoner had been in civil custody for one day and mili- tary custody for 25 days. He was in possession of the South African medal and three clasps,and was entitled to the good conduct badges._

The trial was then con-cluded. At the Barracks on Thursday Dickinson was sentenced to eighty-four days' imprisonment.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH REVISION COURT.

Mr. J. T. Foard, revising barrister, attended at the Conservative Club, Penketh, on Tuesday, for the pur- pose of revising the voters' lists of Penketh, Great Sankey, Cuerdley, and Bold. There were also present Messrs. W. HoHandl Owen, and William A. Maw&ley, representing Unionist interests. Mr. William Shaw (Wigan). representing Mr. Sam Woods, Labour candi- date for the division; and the assistaat overseers, Messrs. E. Foster (Penketh), J. Smith (Great Sankoy), J.Houghton iBold), and J. Wright (Cuerdley). The business was mainly of a routine character. A new claim by Mr. John Houghton. Broom Cottaga, -Great Sankey, was allowed without objection. A claim by Mr. James White, jun., Penketh, was dis- allowed, the assistant overseer stating that in this case the father was upon the list and the son was not quail- _Red.

At the conclusion of the business a discussion took place as to the entering of those in Division 3, also in Division 1. The Revising Barrister pointed out that it was pre- scribed in the precept of assistant overseers that owners entered in Division 3 should also be entered in Division

1. Mr. Shaw pointed out that if this was not done and an ownership claim Nvas struckont a qualified ratepayer might be precluded from a vote, unless his name were entered in Divison 1 also. He also pointed out that at Ashton owners had only been entered in Division 3, and therefore, they had to make claims on behalf of some of the owners to provide for the event of their being disqualified on the ownership claim. The Revising Barrister expressed surprise that the assistant overseer of Ashton should do this, and if it was so he would be getting himself into serious trouble.

THE ALLEGED BANK FRAUD.

Prisoner Committed for Trial.

George Ernest Morris, a native of Exeter,was charged on remand at the Wari%ington Police court on Thursday with obtaining by means of false pretences a gold watch and chain of the value of £2O from Thomas Doraville, jeweller and pawnbroker, Kiautsforil road, on September 4th.

-The Chief oonstable said the case would have to go for trial at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. He would have preferred it to be 'dealt with by their worships that morning but as prisoner had already been convicted several times on indictment the clerk advised that the case ought to go to the Ses- sions. About one o'clock on the date in question prisoner went to the Warrington branch of the National Provincial bank in Bridge-street and told Mr. Meldrum, the manager, that he had an account of upwards of £BOO at Barclay's Bank, Middlesbrough, which he wished to transfer to the National Provincial bank. As prisoner appeared to be very respectable and his address was everything that could be desired, Mr. Meldrum placed faith- in what he said and the prisoner tendered a cheque for £BOO from a cheque book which he had in his possession for Messrs. Barclay and Company's bank at Middlesbrough. The cashier at the Middlesbrough Bank would be called and he would tell the magistrates that at no date had the prisoner any account whatever at that bank, although he attempted a fund there in July last. When the cheque was pre- sented by the prisoner he was told that the usual pro- cedure would be gone through in the transfer- ence of the account. He then asked Mr. Meldrum to cash a small cheque, but Mr. Meldrum very naturally declined to do anything of the kind. Prisoner than said he had made a purchase from a pawnbroker in the town and asked for a blank cheque form. He was supplied with one and went away. He next turned up at Mr. Doraville's shop and procured a gold watch and guard of the value of upwards of £2O. In payment for the articles he tendered the blank cheque which he filled up in the shop. This was subsequently paid into the bank by Mr. Domville and returned to him in due course endorsed"no account." Prisoner had left the town but information .was given to the police and descriptions and photographs of the man were circulated all over the country. The result was that prisoner was apprehended at Chester where he tried tolcommit a similar fraud, and brought to War- rington by Sergeant Williamson. The warrant takenl out by the prosecutor was read over to him and he re- plied Yes. That is right. I intend to plead guilty." Evidencebearing out this statement was then given. It was also stated that prisoner pledged the guard at Mr. Houghton's pawnshop, Warrington, and disposed of the watch at a Liverpool pawnshop. Ernest John Norman Thomas, cashier at Messrs. Bar- clay and Co.'s bank,Middlesbroug'h,said prisoner had no account there on September 4th. He never had an account at the bank. On July 27th he attempted to open an account there by stating that he wished to transfer an account of £6OO from a Preston bank. It was subsequently found that he had no account at Preston. Prisoner was supplied with one of Messrs. Barclay's blank cheque books. Sergeant Williamson said that when apprehended prisoner had Mr. Domville's receipted bill for the watch and guard in his possession, and also the pawn ticket for the watch.

Prisoner said nothing in answer to the charge. He, however, complained to the magistrates that before he had been in the Warrington Police Station two minutes he was taken before the Chief Constable who asked him a great many questions and !took the replies] down in writing. He made him (prisoner) give an account of lie doings from the timehe left school. "IQ you call that justioe,"said the prisoner, "I'm a Dutchman." The Chief Constable: Then you are a Dutchman. (Laughter.) The Chairman said that from what he had heard, the more the police heard about prisoner's antecedents the better it would be for the public.

The Chief Constable saki he told prisoner he was perfectly free to tell him anything or nothing. Prisoner was committed for trial at Liverpool Quarter S essions.

THE TORTURE CHAMBER.

The torture chamber of medileval days was a terrible place, now, fortunately, no longer in existence. But even yet, torture is not unknown in some parts of England. A remarkable case is reported from Birming- ham. Miss Morrie Clarke, of Penn-street, says that until she tried Chas. Forde's.bile beans for biliousness eatingalways brought on torture, for she was a martyr to those terrible ailments, constipation anal indigestion. To a representative of the "Birmingham Telegram " she said :

"Twelve months ago I found my health impaired through constipation. I began to have indigestion and painful stifling sensations after meals, which were brought on really by constipation. All this time I was in agony through constipation, and though it was clear that the best hope of removing the other ailments was to get rid of that terrible cause of them, nothing I could get in the way of medicine gave me any permanent relief.

"When I became so very weak that I found myself troubled with dizziness and fainting upon the least exertion, I placed myself in the hands of a doctor. He gave me every care, but failed to permanently remove the constipation. "Some months ago a friend pressed me hard to try bile beans. I did so, and the effect of taking them was astonishing. I quickly improved. The constipation began to disappear, and after its removal the other ail- ments soon got less and less troublesome. I have now been transformed into a strong, healthy, hearty young woman. lam in excellent health now,never felt better or happier, and I have to thank bile beans alone for the blessing of it all." Bile Beans may be obtained from all chemists, in boxes, at one-and-three-halfpence or two-and-ninepence. The latter size contains three times the quantity of the former. Don't take sub- stitutes.

• Temperance Work. Mr. Henry Taylor, the energetic Police Court Missionary, addressed the first adult meeting in con- , nection with St. Peter's, Bryn, branch of the C.E.T.S., ' held on Monday evening. The Rector who presided afterwards, asked for volunteers to join the society, and in response twenty adults came forward and signed the total abstinence pledge. Mr. Tayldr also gave one of his favourite recitations. A very hearty vote of thanks was given to him with a request that he would soon favour the society again.

A CURE FOR DIABETES.

Daniel Healey, of 113, Lower Oxford-street, Swansea, has published a statement which is completely verified, and which will come as quite a surprise to many who think that Diabetes is an incurable disease. He says "I suffered from Diabetes, and was twice in the hospital, where they treated me for that disease. Each time I came out no better than Iwent in. I had several doctors attend me, and each did his best, but they all failed to iind a cure for my trouble. I had to give up my work, and I thought I should never return to it again. I was advised to try Dodd's Kidney Pills, and the first box did me so much good that I was surprised at the result. I persevered with Dodd's Kidney Pills, and I am pleased to say that they have quite cured me, and lam now back at work. All my friends and fellow workmen are surprised at seeing me well again. I tell them it is entirely due to Dodd's Kidney Pills." There can be no doubt of the truth of this straight- forward statement. Diabetes can be cured. Dodd's Kidney Pills will cure it, and a medicine that will cure diabetes will certainly cure any disease of the Kidneys.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner Saturday, September 26, 1903.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
lace was covered with .matted and tangled hair. His arms and legs, which were bare from the knees downwarth.,. were brown and shaggy. He was clothed in a rude jerkin and knee breeches fashioned out of the skin of some animal, probably a horse or a cow. Tredegar saw now why in the darkness he had mistaken him for a wild beast. But in the light of the lantern he looked very human. His face, though it was 'terribly battered and lisfigured, was almost pathetic in the repose of unconsciousness. Tredegar was glad that .ne eyes were closed.

His mind, however, was made up. He did not stop to think that he was going to destroy the evidence of .his own innocence. He only saw that the earth must be rid of this monster, that the weary soul must be freed from its loathsome prison house, and that Cynthia must never know.

He grasped him round the body, and lifting him from the ground, staggered to the edge of the gulf. He did not know how far the water was below him. He hesitated, and the spray dashed up in his face. Then Heatherbutt sighed and opened his eyes. Tredegar hesitated no longer, and swinging the great body back, he cast it forward into the darkness of the gulf. There - was a heavy splash and a shower of water flew up into his face. The surface could not have been more than three or four feet below the edge. He knew he had done right, but he knew also that the dull sound of that splash, and that faint sigh, and that burst of spray against his face would haunt him to the day of his death. He reeled away from the edge, and went back to Mavanwy. "We must get out of here," he cried hoarsely. " I have done it. It had to be done. You do not know all. Let us go. There must be some way out of this place." She did not answer, but began to move across the slippery ,rock by his side, He held out his hand to help her, but she shrank from him, as though his touch would defile her. The agony of his mind outweighed the bodily pain that he suffered, yet he knew that he had acted rightly. They stumbled on over the rocks till they reached the wall of the cavern, and proceed- ing along by this, they came in time to a ledge, where they found a fresh store of provisions, another lantern, candles, an Unopened bottle of brandy, and a few long strips of some dull grey metal. Tredegar put the candles in his pocket, and pushed the cork V of +he bottle in with his finger. Aftet offering the brandy to Mavanwy, who just touched it with her lips, he took a deep draught himself, and picking up the pieces of metal, examined them. A sudden idea struck him, and he put a lighted match to the end of one of therm In an instant the strip flared up in an intense white light, and a vast panorama of wet rock and glistening wall, and vaulted roof sprang suddenly into view. The light burned fiercely for nearly a minute, and then the whole scene

CHAPTER XX"V

THE DAY OF RECKONING.

TREDEGAR snatched the lantern from the ground and followed as rapidly as he could. He dared not leave Mavanwy, and was obliged to accommodate his pace to 'her's. They stumbled forward through the deep sand, only catching a glimpse now and then of a dim, brown form flying through the darkness. Walroyd was dodging from pillar to pillar, apparently trying to rejoin Tre- degar, but he found himself intercepted at every turn. ,He hoped, however, that the time gained by these tactics would enable Tredegar to overtake his terrible adversary. But Mavanwy was ,weak and ill, and moved but slowly; and Walroyd found himself being driven farther and farther across the cavern. =.l At last Tredegar saw the wretched man vanish through an opening in the wall. The pursuer was but five yards behind, and a door crashed to in his face. However, it gave with the monster's enormous • weight, and Wairoyd had evidently not had time to turn the key in the lock. Both vanished from sight, and Tredegar heard a scream of terror, which echoed and re-echoed through the cavern till it was magnified into a long wail of agony, rising and falling, and then dying away into silence. Quick as thought, he took the key from the inside .of the lock and thrust it into Mavanwy's hand. "When I have gone through," he said, hurriedly, "lock the door and stay this side, You will be quite safe." • She flung the key from her, and passed through the door.

"I will come with you," she said. "I am not afraid to stay; but I must come with you. _ I could not be on the other side of

ihis door while you are in there with them."

Tredegar did iiot wait to argue, but ran down a narrow, rocky passage and emerged into a great cavern. The light of the lan- tern seemed like a speck in the gloom, and Tredegar could see nothing but a circle of glistening rocks. He heard the swish and gurgle of waters sluicing to and fro, and the air was damp with salt spray. A cool breeze beat against his face, and he heard it whistling and moaning through the cavernous heights above his head. It was a veritable cave of the winds. He stretehed out his left hand and tool< Mavanwy's arm. The wet rocks were slip- pery, and he had to almost lift her over theit uneven surface. He saw nothing of the fugitives, and every sound was drowned by the roar of the wind and the crash of the waters. The lantern flickered, and gave a dull and fitful light. Then suddenly, above all the sounds of the elements, a shriek rang out, followed by another, and by yet another. Tredegar dashed forward, and passing round a large piece of rock, came in view of a sight that for a moment made him stand aghast with horror. A dozen yards away a great brown figure stood upon a sloping ledge of rock. Its left hand was stretched out as though to balance itself. Its right grasped Walroyd by one ankle and swung him round and round above its head, as a boy might wave a handker- chief.

Beyond this awful spectacle the rock seemed' to end, and there was a gulf of blackness: Tredegar thrust the lantern into Mavanwy's hand, and told her to stay where she was and close her eyes. Then he rushed forward. But before he could reach the spot the mon- ster loosed his victim, and Walroyd went spinning out into the darkness beyond. Tredegar heard a dull splash, and the next moment his adversary was at his throat, his foot slipped, and he was borne backwards to the ground.

M-avanwy gave a little shriek of terror, and, falling on her knees, clasped her hands and dared not look at the two figures struggling on the sloping rock. But when she raised her eyes again, Tre- degar had gripped his adversary by the waist and was slowly forcing him over to one side, till the two men were both on the ground, and were locked in so close an embrace that their heads hung over each other's shoulders. Then commenced a contest so frightful in its fierceness and intensity that it seemed to Mavanwy's terrified gaze as though no human strength could withstand the strain. For twenty minutes each of the combatants tried to get the other underneath him, and shift his hands from the waist to the throat, without losing title advantage that a firm hold on the body gave to him. But directly either of them tried to effect this, the other man swung him downwards with a crash on the rock, and the positions were reversed. Once Tredegar got a firm grip on his adver- sary, and his powerful fingers clutched the sinewy throat with such strength that Heatherbutt's eyes began to start from their sockets. But the latter, in his brutish mad- ness, drew Tredegar down to him and fas- tened his teeth in the young Welshman's shoulder, literally biting out layers of cloth and flannel and flesh. Tredegar shifted his hold to the body with a superhuman effort, and struggled slowly to his feet, drawing his adversary with him from the ground.

Then he tried to hurl Heatherbutt from his • foothold, hoping to dash him down with such violence as to render him in- sensible; but the madman seemed to be endowed with supernatural strength, and his bare feet gave him a firm foothold on the rock. Tredegar tried in vain to swing him off his balance. The man seemed as strong and resilient as a bar of steel. He bent to and fro under the terrible pressure, but he would not break or fall.

Then suddenly, ,as though by mutual con- sent, both men loosed their hold at the same time, in the hope of obtaining a more advan- tageous grip. For a brief .moment they faced each other, panting, trembling with the strain, one of them foaming at the mouth, and the other with the lust of blood written large on his honest countenance, their eyes glittering with an evil light, their hands crooked like claws, as though they would tear the flesh off each other's bones.

Then Heatherbutt sprang at his opponent with a savage howl, Tredegar met the on- slaught as a rock might meet the foaming fury of the sea, and drove his clenched fist into the man's face with such force that Heatherbutt went reeling backwards to the ground and slid a dozen feet over the surface of the rock.

Tredegar sprang forward to grasp his adversary before the latter could rise to the ground, but Heatherbutt was too quick for him, and was on his feet to meet him. The blood poured from the madman's nose and mouth, and the yellow hair was streaked and splashed with crimson stains. In the dim light the face looked horrible and shapeless. Tredegar drew back his great fist and struck again with all his strength. But this time he struck the empty air. Heatherbutt swerved' with incredible swiftness, and flinging his arm round Tredegar's thighs, almost threw the latter from his .balance. Then Tredegar leant over the shaggy, brown back, and plac- ing his hands underneath the body, joined them, and tried to throw his opponent back- wards over his head. But the effort was too great; the other flung his weight downwards and curved his body like a steel bow. Then they both lost their balance, and came heavily to the ground. And for a whole hour this terrible combat continued to the music of the howling wind and the roaring water. It raged with varying turns of fortune, now one, now the other seeming to have the advantage. Both were men of such gigantic strength that a casual onlooker would have been unable

to gauge the enormous power of their efforts trom the results of their- exertions! Tretteg-ar was undoubtably the superior in strength, but Heatherbutt's ohenomenal activity. and

BY HARRIS BURLAND, AUTHOR OF bACOBRg, MC.

tie firm foothold his bare feet gave him on the rock, and above all the supernatural frenzy of his madness equalised any advantage Tredegar might have gained from sheer weight and muscle. The battle was waged over a large area, but each instinctively carried the, scene ot the contest as far as possible from the edge of the gulf. At one moment it was fought silently and almost without movement on the ground. At another both men were on their feet, tugging and straining to dash each other down to the rock; at another they would lose their grip and beat at each other's faces and bodies; Tredegar with clenched ..

fists, and Heatherbutt with-talons like fingers that left long lines of blood and tattered shreds of clothing wherever they reached their mark.

It was impossible that such a contest could East. It was almost incredible that it had lasted so long. Every minute their move- ments grew more slow, and their efforts more feeble. Their breath came and went in great gasping sobs. They were fighting for their lives, and the first to grow weary must die. And all the while Ma:vanwy watched them with white, face and wide open eyes, crouching by the lantern, and moving it as the scene of the combat shifted, so that the light should always be upon them. She was like a limelight operator following the movements of the actors on the stage. At last the end came. Tredegar's superior strength began to tell, and the wild frenzy ..)f the madman was burning itself out into a dull and stolid resistance. The grasp of the great hairy hands was growing more feeble, the spring of the ,steel muscles less elastic and powerful, the untrained fury of the wild beast was less quick to respond to the schemes and tricks of the human being. Now and again a horrible groan broke from Heather- butt's lips, and when he should have lain still and held tight, he writhed and bit, and tore savagely with his nails. Tredegar, in spite of his terrible exertions and the pain he suffered from his wounds, still kept his wits about him, waiting for the moment when a supreme effort would give him the victory. And he did not Wait in vain. Heatherbutt's strength exhausted it- self in a long and terrible outburst of fury, and then suddenly died down, like the last flicker of a candle. Tredegar felt the slacken-

CHAPTER XXVI. THE POOL.

FOR a few seconds both were too astonished and stunned to move. A dense cloud of sulphurous smoke drifted down on them, and they heard the roar of the waves suddenly die away into silence. Tredegar thought that the explosion had deafened him, but when he had kicked the rock with the heel of his boot, and heard the sound clearly, he realised that something else must have happened. He groped for the lantern and re-lit it. The air was thick with vapour, and he could not see two yards in any direction. He rose to his feet and looked for Mavanwy. She was sitting in a half dazed condition and staring round her as though trying to make out what had happened. She was coughing violently as the pungent smoke rolled past her. Trede- gar went to her side and helped her to her feet.

"What is it? " she said faintly. "I don't know,", he replied. "But the burning wire evidently lit a fuse. Some con- trivance of Walroyd's, I expect. Perhaps an arrangement to blast down the entrance to the cavern. If so, Heaven help us, for we are rats in a trap." He lit a piece of magnesium wire, and the bright light illuminated the smoke till it looked like the drifting of white clouds. Then he looked at the pool, and gave an exclama- tion of Surprise. The narrow belt of sand had disappeared, and the water was lapping against the rock. In an instant he realised the situation, and grasped Mavanwy by the arm.

"The water is rising," he cried; "quick, we must find the entrance."

They hurried off over the rocks till they reached the wall of the cavern. In a few minutes they had followed it up to the narrow passage which led to the doorway. They hurried down this, and then Tredegar, who was in front, stopped and moved his lantern to and fro, examining something that was in front of him. The roof of the passage had caved in, and great pieces of rock were piled up from floor to roof. He seized a huge piece in his hands and dragged it from the pile. Then he flung half a dozen others to the ground. Then a great mass of slate seven feet high confronted him. He grasped it with both hands and tugged at it with all his strength, till he tore small pieces out of its edges. But it refused to budge an inch. Walroyd had laid the mine too well. They were indeed, as Tredegar had said, rats in a trap, He looked at Mavanwy, who leant against the wall of the passage trembling. "We must go back." he cried. "It will be all right, Mavanwy. I don't suppose the water will rise very much." But when they reached the place they had just left, the water was on a level with the top of the rock, and in some places it had begun to pour over the edge.

For a few seconds they watched it in silence, ,Mavanwy half paralyzed with fear and• Tredegar swiftly planning out some means of escape. At last a stream trickled over the soles of their boots, and a broken plank slid half over the edge of the rock. Tredegar seized it and drew it on one side. He had decided on a course of action, and set to work at once.

He moved quickly along the rock, splashing through the water as he went, and gathered together every suitable plank and spar that was within reach. Then, stripping off the cordage, he started to bind them together into some sort of raft. He made the frame- work of four large spars, crossed them with ,a number of smaller ones, and laid planks on the top of these. He worked quickly and without much attention to detail, but before he had finished the water was over their knees. The raft, however, was a solid piece of work. It was about twelve feet square, and looked substantial , enough to support several people. .

Then-he-picked up Mavanwy, and, placing her in the centre, climbed on himself. One side of the little craft sank into the water with his great weight; but when they had " trimmed " the boat, it floated well, and the planks were quite !Ai inches above the surface.

"This is our only chance," Tredegar said. "It is possible the water may not rise much further and that it may subside." , "Supposing the -cavern lies below the level of the sea ?" Mavanwy said. "What then ?"

Tredegar's face Whitened, and he gripped one of the spars so tightly that his nails made dents in the wood.

"What then ?" he replied, slowly. "Well, Mavanwy, we shall have to face it. For me it •does not matter, now that I seem to have lost your love. I can face death without fear,_ My only sorrow will be for you."

"I, too, Can face death Without fear, Emrys,?" she said. "And I would rather die here with you than live the life that fate has marked out for me. But it is no time yet to talk of death, Emrys." And she smiled bravely through the tears that came to her eyes.

He took her hinds in his and raised them to his lips. She did not resist. She did not look upon it as an act of love. "There is no need to talk of it, Mavanwy," he said, after a pause. "It is visible and before our eyes. If the top of this cavern lies below the level of the sea, the cavern will gradually fill up. Evidently the pas- sage through which the waters poured out has been stopped up. Probably the concus- sion of the explosion has shaken down a huge mass of rock. • If the cavern lies only partly below the level of the sea, the water will rise to that point and remain there, only rising and falling with the tides. In either event the same thing will happen. But in the one case it will come more quickly. No, Mavanwy, there is no need to talk of death. It will speak to us soon enough." She was silent. An hour, passed, and neither of them spoke, but Tredegar still held her hand. The waters rose slowly, inch by inch, and the frail craft swam round and round in the circling current. Planks, barrels, spars, and all the debris that is shorn off a broken vessel by the sea floated past them in endless succession. Once part of a skeleton still lashed to a spar spun round and round by their side, till Tredegar lifted it from the water and flung it far away from them. Forttrnately, no vessels had survived the storms that had brought them to the pool. Their shattered hulks lay now many feet under the water. If any of them had floated on the surface they would have added another terror to the perilous voyage of the raft, and it might have been crumpled up between two hulks like a walnut between a hammer and anvil.

Tredegar watched the various objects go by, and he idly thought of Poe's story of the maelstrom, and wondered if these articles would obey the laws of motion laid down by that master of imagination. Now and again he would make a note of some mark on the wall of the cavern, and then look again for it when the raft passed it in its next revolution; more often than not it would have vanished. He idly speculated how long the water would take to reach the roof. So far •it was not visible in the lamplight. Then slowly something else circled out of the darkness and fidated into the light of the lantern—a frail plank clutched by a pair of bloodless hands, and a dim, white face look- ing upwards through the water. It was John Walroyd ! It was evident that he was dead, for his mouth was below the surface. But he still held the plank in the grip of death. Tredegar could See a long gash across his forehead. Probably the wretched man had struck some plank or spar when Ileatherbutt had flung him from the edge of the rock. Mavanwy, did not see his ghastly face, and Tredegar was glad when the grim vision was carried away once more into the surrounding gloom.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
Then commenced a contest so frightful in its fierceness and intensity that it seemed to Mavanwy's terrified gaze as no human strength could withstand the strain.

Ing of the grasp, and the labour of the other's breathing. Both were on the ground locked in a close embrace. Then Tredegar suddenly loosed his opponent's body, and placing his hands on the madman's chest, thrust himself upwards with such force .that he broke from his opponent's clasp. Then he gripped him by the throat, and exerting all his great

sii-ength he lifted Heatherbutt's head irom the rock, arid daslled it down with all his force. He lifted it again; and yet again, beating the ground with it tel the rock was red with blood. Then Heatherbutt lay still, with closed _.eyes and wide open mouth. The com- bat was over:

Tredegar staggered to his feet and gazed blindly at IClavanwy through a mist of blood. Then he reeled and fell crashing to the rock. He struggled up on to one elbow and cried out to her. As she advanced towards him the yellow light seemed to fade away in a grey fog, and then all was darkness. When he came to his senses, he found Mavanwy bending over him and wiping the blood from his face with a damp handkerchief. His brain was dizzy and the dar,k outline of rocks seemed to spin slowly round him.. "Are you better, dear Emrys ? " she said, and her voice sounded like a far off echo.

"I am better," he said, passing his hand across his forehead. " I expect I have lost some blood."

"I have bound up the wound on your shoulder," she replied. "I think it will be all right. The others are only scratches, but they must hurt terribly."

"- I think it was the strain of the long struggle more than anything else," he said. "I have always reckoned myself a strong man, Mavanw3r, but I would not go through a fight like that again for all the wealth in the world. Is he dead ? "

"I don't think so," she answered, "he has not moved but he is breathing faintly." Tredegar struggled to his feet. He must die," he said quietly. "It would be crueltl to let him live, .and it is better he should die now while he is unconscious." "Oh, Emrys," she cried out in ' horror, "you could not do it." I must, I must, IVlavanwy. It is im- possible to let him loose once more on the earth." "Bind him," she answered, "and have him taken to an asylum."

" It is 'better for him to die," Tredegar said slowly. " I will throw him over the edge of the gulf. Stay where you are, Mavanwy, and do not look."

"I cannot, I -cannot," she cried. " Emrys, it is murder." "It is a kindness. He, would thank me„ Mavanwy —his poor soul would thank me for releasing it from this living death."

He took the lantern and strode forward towards the prostrate form of Heatherbutt, and Mavanwv stood rooted to the ground in horror) Then she turned her head away and buried her face in her hands.

Tredegar stooped down and peered into the face of the unconscious madman. It was the first time he had had a clear view of him in the light, and he seemed horribly human. His

died away, and the yellow flicker of the lantern seemed a mere mockery of the darkness.

"Magnesium wire," said Tredegar, thrust- ing the remaining strips into his pocket. "It may be useful." Then they continued their search in silence, still .keeping to the wall. But as they proceeded the splash of the waters grew more distinct, and a faint spray began to drift against their faces.

_ All at once a line -of darkness crossed the wet rock in front of' them, and they found themselves on the edge, of the pool. Tredegar held, the lantern so that its rays shone down on the water, and he saw a wide swift stream 'pouring past him like a mill race. And as far as the eye could reach, a thick mass of wreck- age was jammed together and heaped up above the level of the stream. The water flew into the air as it dashed against great spars, and keels, and ribs of vessels, and it roared unceasingly. Then Tredegar knew that this was the place that Walrbyd had spoken of—the grave of a thousand vessels, ancl ten thousand men, and it was from here that Temawr had gathered his jewels and his dead.

They walked along the edge of the—stream, till they had passed the place where Heather. butt had been flung out to his death. Tredegar looked carefully for his victim's body, but could see no signs of it. Then fifty yards further on the lantern showed a great barrier of broken rock and wreckage heaped up to a height of over twenty feet above the surface of the water. Tredegar lit another piece of magnesium wire, and in the dazzling light they saw a strange scene. On the other side of the barrier lay a smooth black pool of water, dotted as far as the eye could reach with the remnants of shattered vessels. Broken masts rose thirty feet from the surface, and rotting rope hung in shreds from their spars. Here and there the whole long hull of a vessel showed above the waters, -or a line of bare ribs curved up from the depths below. Loose planks, barrels, and spars spun round and round in a circle, and there was not a clear piece of water ten feet square to be seen any- where. On one of the vessels they could see the skeletons of dead men, still set in the attitude in which they had died. Beneath their feet lay a small margin of smooth sand. An iron- bound box gaped open, and from the rent in its side a stream 'of yellow coins had poured out on the shore. It could not have floated to the beach, and Tredegar wondered whose hand brought it there and who had commenced to ransack its treasures. Not five yards away from them lay the bones -of a man, and a few coins still lay among the slender outlines of his fingers. All this Tredegar and Mavanwy saw in the space of a minute. Then the blazing wire flared down close to Tredegar's fingers, and he flung the burning piece upon the grounds As he did so, a light spurted up under his feet, and a second later a swift sputtering flame ran through the. darkness like a long thin serpent of fires Then fifty yards away there was a blinding flash, a long dull roar, and a shock that extinguished the light of the lantern, and threw both of them to the round.

were more or

less expected. After the speeches which Mr. Ritchie. had -made it was felt by everybody that there was no seat for him in a Balfourian Cabinet. He had directly attacked the root of his leader's whole policy: and one or other had to go. The other names of the ministers, who handed in their iiortfolios, were pronounced " free traders," and, especially after Mr. Balfour's now famous grey pamphlet, they had to go. Following immediately upon the resigna- tions of Mr. Chamberlain, Mr. Ritchie, and Lord George Hamilton, Secretary for India, were those of Lord Balfour of Burleigh, Secretary for Scotland, and the Hon. A. Elliot, Financial Secretary to the Treasury. Lord Balfour of Burleigh was Parliamentary Secretary to the Board of Trade from 1888 to 1892. When Lord Salisbury formed his Ad- ministration in. 1895 he was promoted to be Secretary for Scotland, and given Cabinet rank, and he retained that office at the re- constitution of the Ministry after the general election of i9oo, and also when Mr. Balfour became Prime Minister on the retirement of Lord Salisbury 14 months ago. The Hon. Arthur Ralph Douglas Elliot, second son of the third Earl of Minto, has only been in the Ministry for a brief period, having been chosen by Mr. Balfour during last session to be Financial Secretary to the Treasury on the resignation of Mr. Hayes Fisher.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW MEMBER FOR ST. ANDREWS.

St. Andrews at this time of the year sug- gests golf rather than politics; but last week the latter temporarily ousted even the former. At a critical time like the present, the Govern- ment always endeavours not to vacate a seat unless compelled to on account of the bye- election at- tached to it; yet at the same time no member can retain his seat upon ap- pointment to an office from which he draws a salary. So with his new post as a direc- tor of the Suez Canal, Mr. An- struther, the Conservative Whip, had to resign his seat in the House of Commons, and this rendered a bye-election necessary at St. CAPTAIN ELLICE, ndrews, which vas fought with -plenty of spirit on both sides. The candi- dates were Major Anstruther Thompson, the •well-known sportsman, and Captain Ellice, Who stood in the Liberal interest; and the result, if not very decisive—a small majority being turned into a small minority—went against the present Administration. Of -course, the great bone of contention was the question of the country's fiscal policy; but the election was, so to speak, fought in the dark, as it was previous to Mr. Balfour's apologia and Mr. Chamberlain's resignation.

THE ROCHESTER LIBERAL.

Not many men who have contested a seat in the House of Commons have had such an interesting life as Sir Harry Johnston. "My life," he says, "has been all adventure." By the time he was twenty-two he had visited :the greater part of Europe and much of North Africa. Then he trav- elled through West Africa and and the regions of the Congo,

and at the early age of twenty- six he was ap- pointed in 1884 to lead the Kilima - Njaro Expedition, at the close of which he con- cluded those treaties which laid the founda- tion of our East African protec- torate. Iri 1889.-

THE ST. PAUL'S TRIP.

Mr. Spencer has undoubtedly made a step forward in the question of aerial navigation, although he did not fully accomplish his intention to sail round St. Paul's. The wind was against him, and the wind riqw seems to be the one factor in the great problem which the various inventors are unable to cope with. Till they do of course there will be very little practical value in an airship; but Mr. Spencer has shown a great advance in his machine and his handling of it. Before he made his real attempt, he showed the public at the Crystal Palace that he had control over his airship, and the steering, whiCh has always been the chief thing which has prevented inventors from perfecting (heir machines, was splendid.

THE SPENCER AIRSHIP.

In fact, Mr. Spencer sailed quite comfortably and easily from the suburbs to the heart of London, but when he, got to his objective point he found the wind too strong for him, and he was carried up north. This he ascribes to the fact that his airship is experimental and is worked by quite a weak engine. Had the engine been sufficiently powerful, he claims that lie could have accomplished his purpose by driving into the teeth of the wind; but with only a twenty-four horse-power motor he could make no impression upon the elements, which were most pronouncedly hostile. • HoW- ever, it is quite likely that by the time that these lines are in print Mr. Spencer will have made another and a successful effort to sail round St. Paul's. It all depends on the wind.

THE NEW CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER,

Mr. Austen Chamberlain, who is to succeed Mr. Ritchie, is only forty years of age, and he has been eleven years in Parliament as the representative of East Worcestershire. During the short Liberal Administration of 1892-1895 he served as one of the junior Whips of the Unionist party. His maiden

speech in Parlia- ment was made

on the Home

Rule Bill of 1893,

and won:for him

a compliment

from Mr. Glad-

stone, which is not likely to be forgotten. "It was a speeeh,"

observed the veteran Liberal leader,"that must have been dear and refreshing to a father's heart."

It was also a

speech that proved to the country that the young member THE RIGHT HON. J. AUSTEN was destined to CHAMBERLAIN,'M.P. attain a distin- guished place. - - -- • • • •

On the return of the Unionists to power in 1895 Lord Salisbury appointed him to- the office of Civil Lord of the Admiralty, where he served under Mr. (now Lord) Goschen. After the General Election of 1990 he became Financial Secretary to the Treasury, a post he filled until offered the Postmaster-Generalship in succession to Lord Londonderry. Whilst holding this office, Mr. Austen Chamberlain represented the Colonial Office in the House of Commons during his father's absence in South Africa. His admirers say there can be no doubt that if circumstances had permitted him to remain longer at the head of the Post Office he would have left the mark of his administrative capacity on this important branch of the public service., But he is now required for a post where his quali- ties will be put to a severer test. The Chancellorship of the Exchequer is the most distinguished Cabinet office next to the Pre- miership, and has frequently been held by the Prime Minister or by the leader of the House of Commons when the Premier was a member of the Upper House. It is said that, in the opinion of the Prime Minister, Mr. Austen Chamberlain is not only fitted, notwithstand- ing his youth, to fill the most important offices of state, but is marked out aS a future lesliler of the partyi

A NEW BOLERO

or cloth skirt, and I can warmly recommend it for immediate wear to such of my readers as are in doubt as to what to select :or immediate wear.

The newest skirts are nearly all made in the eminently sensible plan which allows them to clear the ground all the way round. They are still made full, some having the fulness all the way round the waist; this being accom- plished by skilfully arranged pleats for the for Morning wear with a plain tweed, serges yoke, for empiecement is now conspicuously absent from all the best models. Other skirts again have the fulness kept more to the back, but the skirt is so well cut that it stands out well from the waist. The broad flat pleats stitched and pressed into place for some distance below the waist and springing out below the knee are perhaps the most popular for the moment. The coatee sketched shows one of the latest and smartest styles for one ..)f the little coats which form so important and necessary an item of our outfit at present. The bolero proper will not remain much longer as an every day piece of dress, for as the season, advances we require something a little more clinging and warm, and the .smart little garment sketched is• eminently designed to, suit these requirements. Added warmth is

AN AUTUMN HAT:

given to the shoulders by the double cape, ana the chest is protected by the embroidered cloth vest fastened down the front with small gold buttons. The back and sides of the coat are tight fitting and held in place by the wide folded silk band, while the frpnts hang loosely. There are twists of cord afid • tassels arranged to give, a finish down either side of the fronts. This coat would look well expressed in black cloth with a white cloth vest embroidered in coloured silk and a closely fitting band of black velvet.

The hat sketched is an exceedingly pretty style for autumn wear. It is made of burnt straw and is trimmed with a wreath of cherries and then leaves arranged round the crown. It is of the wide flat shape which still holds its own, although there are hints abroad as to a marked change from this plateau style to more pronounced crowns of the jam-pot order. There is generally in the order of things a rebound from any very pronounced fashion which is apt to lead us to extremes, but it is to be hoped that this will not be so as regards our millinery for autumn and winter. , Extremes of any fashion are alv.-ai-s avoided by a woman with really good taste who, while following the ever varying modes closely, will alway 3 be - guided by her innate sense of fitness. . Toques are certainly becoming less aggressive than hitherto, and will worn smaller and of a neater shape. Bright. colours will be much used in hats and toques and sometimes inter- mingled so as to introduce a brilliant combina- tion of colouring into our tout ensemble. The Examiner, Saturday, September 26, 1903.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

The past week has, politically speaking, been the most sensational since the Home Rule crisis of seventeen years ago. Of late years politics 'have often appeared to be a dull sort of game; 'but they have suddenly jumped into prom- inence and interest in a very unusual way. Probably there has never been a greater sur-

prise in politics than Mr.. Cham- berlain's resig- nation, which " fell in the political arena like a bomb- shell," -- to quote' a leading speaker on the subject. The other resigna- tions, which paled beside the great surprise, would have by t he m selves formed a crisis in the ordinary way ; but they

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

written many IDOOkS, and eacti is brim- ful of experiences. Describing a visit to his Highness Sayyid Barghash's palace, Sir Harry tells the curious story of . two paintings, full length, and identical, of the Sultan of Zan- zibar, on either side of the mirror. The pictures were manufactured in Paris. The Sayyid had a photograph taken of himself during his visit to London. It was in a sitting posture. He sent it to Paris with the order that it was to be enlarged standing, and then converted into two oil paintings. The Parisian artist, in no way at a loss, cut off the head of the sitting Sultan and stuck it on to the decapitated 'portrait of some Algerian Arab photographed erect. The con-i- -'l4/nation was enlarged, and in due time gave rise to the two oil paintings in the palace of Zanzibar.

September is the first month of the dull season which lies between the confirmation of the summer modes which are at their height in August, while the early winter fashions are still in their infancy, so that there is little to chronicle in the way of novelties. This season the weather has quite distinguished itself by its determination to do us out of the much-desired summer, and rain and cold have prevailed when sun and warmth could be reasonably expected, so that dainty muslin and lace toilettes have not been so much in demand and serge and light woollen materials have bad .an unusual and almost unprece- dented vogue. It has been an altogether dis- appointing state of affairs, for the English winter is generally a somewhat prolonged

misery, and during its dismal reign we must perforce eschew all delicate gowns for out-of- doors and content ourselves with the more serviceable cloth dresses of which we are apt to grow wearied. Just now, however, a variety is offered to relieve the monotony of things generally, for this is the season of the year when tartans and plaids make their annual appearance and prove very becoming and smart looking . when well cut and made. The neat skirt depicted is made of one of the new tartans which are very pretty and soft looking in which different colours, such as rich brown, green, and mauve or blue are inter- mingled. The skirt is cut to walking length and is made with wide flat box pleats. Some- times tartan skirts are trimmed round the lower part of the skirt with strappings or tuck- ings of plain self coloured cloth. The most suitable coat for wear with a 'skirt of this description is tight-fitting and of plain cloth, for however well tartan looks as a skirt it does not look well for a tight-fitting coat. A tartan silk blouse relieved by a dainty lace collar and cravat looks very smart, however, for morning wear with a plain tweed, serge,

Historic Castles and their Heirs:—September has been remarkable for the coming of age of several heirs to peerages. No fewer than five or six celebrations could be mentioned, including the coating of age of Lord Brooke, the elder son of the Earl and Countess of Warwick; and of the Hon. H. C. Vane, Lord Barnard's son and heir. The celebrations at Warwick Castle have been postponed on accoaat of family bereavement, but Raby Castle has been the scene of great festivities in honour of the event. Few places are so rich in historic associations as both Warwick and Raby Ctstles. Lady Warwick has only recently finished her history of the famous Castle,a work upon which she has been engaged for four or five years, and which will be deeply interesting as a record of so many stirring events in English history, At Baby, the gre tt dining hall remains as it was in the old times when the N evilles held the Castle, and in this chamher some six or seven hundred nobles and warriors would sometimes sit down to dinner.

The Duchess of Roxburghe.—The Duchess of Rox- ! burghe intends to go. to America next month to be pre- sent at the wedding of her son and Miss Goelet, which is to take place in New York the second week in November. Of late the Duchess has lived very quietly I at Broxmouth Park, her home in Dunr,"%rtkt a great part of her life was spent at the Court of(QueenVictoria. and she was twice Mistress of the Robps. Her maiden name was Lady Anne Spencer-Churchi I, a daughter of the seventh Duke of Marlborough, an 4 she married the late Duke of Roxburgh° when a girl o 20, and has had a family of seven. The Lake District.—lt has been said tk?.t a visit to the Lake District in the depth of wintel•,is quite as , impressive in its scenery as a visit to summer. But more beautiful than snow-covered fells and mountains are the autumn tints which perhaps can be nowhere seen to better advantage than in the Lake District. There also may be seen from certain points some of the most longdistant views to be obtained in the British Isles. When free from summer haze it is possible to see both Snowdon and.the more distant Ben Lomond, also the Isle of Man, the Morne Mountains in Ireland, and the Isle of Ai ran, several of these places being over 100 miles from the point where they can be viewed.

Health-giving Games.—From time immemorial, man has played with a ball, but until comparatively recent years, women have generally thought that they had more important matters to occupytheir time. The craze for golf, which in reality is only the child's game of fol- lowing a ball round a field for the mere pleasure of hitting it illustrates the saying that once a boyalways a boy, when it is a question of playing games. Some people think, if we may judge from letters appearing in the Press,that it is a sign of degeneration that women should now be equally as ready to indulge in sports and pastimes, but it is quite impossible to agree with this view. Playing with a ball may not be a very intellectual pursuit, butno one can doubt that outdoor exercise has vastly improved the physique of the present generation of women.

Vanity's Antidote.—Possibly one reason why women have been backward to indulge in outdoor games, has arisen from the fear that they do not always look their best in plain and simple garments necessary for the free movement of the limbs. A writer in one of the weekly papers suggests that but for this many more women woull play tennis, hockey and golf, than already do, but it may be doubted whether this is the case. Occasionally one sees a woman playing on the golf links elaborately over dressed, but she is quite the exception, and there are in these days very few women who refuse to indulge in outdoor games simply because they won't be seen in the plain and simple style of dress which is alone suitable for such occasions. The Dress of Women who Work.—No one can doubt that women have become, on the whole, much better dressed since they have generally taken to business life. Except amongst the very- poor, the badly shod, pinned together young person is seldom seen. On the other hand, complaint as often made, that the business girl apes too much the fashions of the leisured classes. Formerly, class dressed as class, now all try to dress as one class,and affectation of fashionable modes is just as pronounced among .business girls, as any others. It is useless to preach against this tendency, but at the same time the girl clerk or typist, whose clothes are well chosen, and scrupulously neat and appropriate, shows better taste than her fellow worker who merely tries to be fashionable. _ _ _

The Autumn Revival.—Fiscal problems and political crises have butllittle effect nowadays upon social affairs. That section of society which moves in Parliamentary circles is now but such a small part of the whole, that the unsettlefflpolitical outlook is not likely to have much effect upon the autumn season in London. It is early yet for the return of fashionable folks, and given fine weather, many hostesses intend if possible to keep their country house parties together for October, but already the West End of London is shewing . sign t of recovery from the stagnation of the holiday season. The shops are exhibiting all their autumn novelties, and their proprietors have been cheered by the recent spell of fine cold weather, which, by searching out little signs of shabbiness, has induced many buyers to sally forth in search of new garments. Furs.—White furs promise to be less in evidence this season than they were last, the reason for this state of affairs probably being that we are all a little tired of white furs and their almost universal unbecomingness. Sable will be the fur of the coming winter, but for those who cannot afford this luxury, there remains moleskin, and for those whose purses do not permit of this now very favourite fur, its clever imitation, made from musguash. Another very excellent substitute for moleskin is moleskin plush, which is so realistic a fraud as to defy the most searching scrutiny. This fabric is of home manufacture, and for pelerines coats, and indeed entire costumes, it seems likely to be extensively used. Autumn Headgear.—The new autumn hats are eithe4 white, or much trimmed with white and velvet flowers and foliage are being much patronised by leading modistes as a trimming. Ostrich feathers which have steadily been growing in width and length, have now reached enormous proportions, and after partly encircl- ing a hat, often fall on the shoulders behind. The more exclusive milliners prefer these plumes to be either in back or white, and unquestionably they are to be pre- ferred. Huge, coloured feathers, however well worn, give always a slight air of vulgarity,andrecall the head- gear of the lowest class of women when Bank Holidays come round.

_ Buttons.—Buttons of every kind and description are much in request, as a trimming, but wherever they are placed they must appear to be of use, and must look as though they fastened something. rhe home-worker will find considerable scope for her ingenuity in the way of fancy buttons. The larger varieties, covered with course crochet, with little ruches of ribbon in the centre, are exceedingly effective and easy of manu- facture, so also are those covered with velvet suede, or a network of gold thread. As is usual at this season of the year, touches of gold appear about many of the newest creations, hence the popularity of the gold button.

MADGE.

"I'll bet you a dollar von did not see me bet!" was the remark of :lames Meadoweroft when a constablo informed him he would be reported for street betting. In an hour and a quarter 51 men and six women had gone up to him in a street at Pendlebury with money. On Wednesday. for the sixth time, he was fined .£5.

LEEDS v. WIDNES.

apply to 36 and 38. Queen Victoria Street. London. Factory —Peckham, S.B. (63.)

These clubs met at Widnes on Saturday in favourable weather, before about 3,000 spectators. Playing with the wind Widnes pressed vigorously, and in a few minutes Tilly had, from a penalty, registered a goal. Ten minutes later, Bown, the home wing three-quarter, had to be carried from the field, his knee having given way. Despite this loss, Widnes continued to press, and play was almost entirely confined to the visitors' terri- tory. Eight minutes before the interval, however,Leeds made a plucky, effort, but half-time arrived with; Widnes 1 goal (2 points) , Leeds. nil. In the second half, with the wind at their backs, the visitors had all theadvantage, mid though the homesters occasionally broke through, they were for the major part compelled to remain on the defensive. Nothing, however, resulted, and, within five minutes of time, the Leeds oontingent looked like being defeated. Suddenly, however, Grace broke through the home defence, and threw to Ward, who transferred to Crampton, and the latter registered a try. which Grace converted. Widnes next made a determined dash, giving the visitors an extremely warm few minutes. The Leeds defence,how- ever, proved invulnerable; and the only advantage which the invaders achieved was a penalty, which was badly mulled. Final: Leeds, 1 goal 1 try (5 points) ; Widnes, 1 goal (2 points.) OLDHAM v. HUDDERSFIELD.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON "A" v. ROCHDALE HORNETS "A."

There was a large attendance at Wilderspool on Saturday to witness the encounter between these teams. Mahoney was an absentee from the Warrington ranks his place being taken by Brooks whilst Jackson played on the wing. Roberts kicked off for Warrington, the visitors having the advantage of the wind in the first half. Soon after the kick off, Townson gained posses- sion but passed clumsily and this enabled Rochdale to make headway. In a short time Smith received the ball and rounding Townson scored the first try for the visitere: The kick at goal was abortive. On resuming the Warrington men rushed into the Rochdale quarters and Townson made a smart effort but was tackled. Play was transferred to the other end and Brooks saved when the visitors became dangerous. Knight mulled and the Rochdale men again got going but Warburtoa kicked dead at the critical moment. Brooks was conspicuous with a fine run, but his opponents proved too many for him. Watkins took a pass from one of the backs near the half-way line, and after a splendid run scored between the posts. Knight kicked a goal. The Warrington men continued to press, and Brooks, obtaining possession from Jordan scored a capital try. Knight again added the extra points.
