Mr. BALFOUR has DOUBTFUL COMFORT reason to be grateful FROM ROCHESTER. just now for any happy accidents that may befall him, and the personal popularity of Mr. TUFF at Rochester is certainly one of them. It is also fortunate for the Primo Minister that the licensed victuallers in that borough resolved to use all their "legitimate influence " on behalf of the Unionist candidate. There is a pleasant anec- dote of Mr. TUFF, that, on the occasion of the dis- tribution of mugs to a gathering of children, he noticed that by some oversight a deserving little girl had been deprived of her mug; whereupon he devo- tedihimself to the redress of this grievance. Early in:tbe contest, this story was revived for Mr. TUFF'S credit, and it seems to have been hailed as an omen by the trade which is legitimately connected with mugs of a larger growth. All the local con- ditions conspired in favour of Mr. BALFOUR'S champion, and probably they helped him consider- ably more than his vague adhesion to the principles of a celebrated pamphlet. Mr. TUFF is in favour of some commercial system Which he calls reciprocity, and against any increase of the cost of living. His election accordingly grati- fies all sections of the Unionist party. They are at least agreed upon one thing—that it is desirable to keep a seat for the Government at any hazard. Argyllshire and St. Andrews were so disquieting that the man whose "legitimate influence is strong enough to hold ROCHESTER is a friend in need, whether he professes any principles or none. If ministers can contrive to carry on, as the " Standard " says they should, without troubling their heads about new fiscal proposals, Mr. TUFF is their man. If Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN should persuade any considerable section of the party that the taxation of food will leave it just as cheap as ever, Mr. TUFF is quite open to conviction. His success will be a great en- couragement to the Unionist partisan who is keenly alive to the personal disadvantages of making a decided stand. The "Morning Post" considers that Rochester has awarded "first honours to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN," And the " Stand- ard " that it has declared for the " normal " policy of letting everything alone. This little difference of opinion must be cheering to Mr. BALFOUR, who wishes to please everybody. It may confirm the DUKE of DEVONSHIRE in the belief which he is said to hold, that the policy of providing some weapon which will be useful in commercial bargaining with foreign Powers, is the adapration of Free Trade to modern needs." Here is a formula which should be serviceable at Sheffield with the help of Mr. T FF.

MR. BALFOLTR'S little THE WOES OF A game of makeshifts PREMIER. is not going very smoothly. The shuff- ling of old Ministers, and the recruiting, of new, are apparently troublesome. Perhaps Me. BROD - RICK refuses to be moved from the War Office, which he has mismanaged, to the India Office, which does not yearn for him. India has done him no harm, and vigorously resents the idea of being made his next victim. Perhaps Lord Cugzon, who has the habit of speaking his mind, has told Mr. BALFOUR very plainly what he thinks of Mr. BROD- RICK. It may be that Lord MILNER is not very eager to enter a Cabinet from which Lord LANs- DOWNE cannot be induced to. budge. Lord MILKER has been on the Continent, and can judge for himself whether Europe has a great respect for the Foreign Sacretary. There is very little in Paris, wheat it is plainly intimated that Mr. BALFOUR'S reformed Cabinet will not sustain the authority of England in the councils of Europe. "In present circumstances," writes the Paris correspondent of the "Times," "England's voice in the concert of the Powers has lost its metallic ring and is reduced to a hearse whisper." What that means the situation in Bulgaria and Macedonia tells us every day. Bulgaria has not yet received a reply from England to her recent Note, and the reply of France is "guarded," though "sym- pathetic." The French Government would probably be willing enough to respond to the

generous sentiment of the French people if there were a strong had at our Foreign Office to keep them company. It is clear that the French have no sympathy with the Eastern policy of their Russian ally. 'f It is a sinister illusion," says the "Temps," "to suppose that it is sufficient to give the SULTAN carte blanche to suppress the insurrec- tion." A still more sinister illusion is reported from Sofia. Count LAMiDORFF has informed the Bulgarian Government that "in the opinion of Count von Biilow, which ho apparently shares, Turkey, as the suzerain Power, has the right to send troops into the vassal Principality for the purpose of preserving ordor." This is what we migtAt expect from the Garman Chancellor. But we also expect the Foreign Minister of England to make it clear to that personage and others that such action on the part of the Sultan would be stArnly discountenanced by this country, and that the strongest measures would be taken to prevent it. To let the Bashi-Bazouks loose in Bulgaria might gratify Cunt von Billow; but it would not be tolerated by common humanity which still has some influence in Europe. Mr. BALrouR needs a Foreign Secretary to impress this elementary truth upon the three Chancellors, and to co-operate with France in a policy which shall make the SULTAN'S rule in Macedonia nothing but a diplomatic figment.

There is no such hope for the Macedonians in the reconstructed Ministry. The hopes of the Unionist party seem to find satisfaction equally remote. What is the basis upon which a politician can conscientiously join Mr. BALFOUR in his new enterprise? Mr. ARTHUR ELLIOT ex- plains that he resigned because he could not acquiesce in a policy which is shaping for the taxation of bread and meat, butter and cheese. We can fancy Mr. BALFOUR exclaiming in his airy way: "What a sensitive conscience that makes such a fuss about cheese !" The " Standari" re- bukes Mr. ELLIOT for allowing cheese to weigh so heavily on his chest. "We cannot help thinking that he might well have waited .to S3O whether there is any chance that his fears are likely to be realised." Very well; that looks like a definite understanding which even the Free Trade Unionist may accept without risk. An ambitious statesman, solicitous to enter the Government, may say to Mr. BALFOUR "All right, I'll come in on your specific assurance that cheese is not to be disturbed. But as soon as ho has committed himself to that his eye may fall upon this alarming passage in the " Standard:" "Mr. BALFOUR should seek means to repudiate he insinuation that the retirement of Mr. CHAM- BERLAIN was really intended to work an arrange- ment for the ultimate adoption of the policy which the Cabinet is supposed to have discarded." So Mr. ELLIOT'S scruples were not so unreasonable after all ! "It is difficult to believe that Mr. BALFOUR Will be content to rest under so improbable and diskonouring an imputation." That incorrigible humorist, Mr. GIBSON BOWLES, puts the dilemma in another way. Either the Cabinet is Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S washpot, the Edom over which he has cast out his shoe, although the foot is withdrawn, or else Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBER- LAIN to become CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER has committed political parricide. The humorous eye of Mr. BOWLES sees the ruthless son planting a dagger in the father's heart. And yet Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, as he wraps his toga over„his face, after the manner of the dying CAESAR, does not cry." Et tu Austen!" This bit of drama calls for ' explanation at Sheffield, although the exact terms on which Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN remains in the Cabinet did appear to be candidly admitted in a famous correspondence. But that is what the "Standard " calls" an improbable and dishonour- ing imputation." Our contemporary's innocence is really too childlike for this wicked world!

Warrington is the poorer by the death of Mr, Thomas Fletcher. He was the captain of an in- dustry which it may be said,he created.lle possessed in a high degree the rare quality of being able to apply scientific knowledge to practical everyday uses. To him the Norld at large is greatly indebted for the scientific and domestic applica- tion of gas as a fuel. His discovery of the lawt ruling the forms and proportions of burners con- suming gas and air mixtures led to the use of gas in the home for heating and cooking purposes. In the outside world his labour-saving appliances in this connection have proved a boon to every dentist and to chemists in chemical and physical laboratories, whilst business men generally have profited by them. Municipal gas undertakings and gas companies have been enriched by his popular- isation of the use of coal gas as a fuel. It is a coincidence of more than passing moment that th,e town which was the scene of Joseph Priestley s discovery of oxygen and other important dis- coveries in chemistry should also be the home of one who turned to practical account those dis- coveries. Like Priestley too, Mr. Fletcher was a voluminous writer on scientific subjects and his standard works on "Dental Metallurgy" and "Coal Cris as a Fuel" will long remain a me- morial of him. Unlike many scientific men Mr. Fletcher reaped the regard of his labours in seeing bis efforts successful and fully appreciated. Fortunately, his success never spoiled him but rather tended to give his charitable and generous disposition full play. Warrington is unable to claim him as one of her own sons but she may well be proud of having had such a citizen in her midst for 40 years.

Public interest will doubtless be aroused to a considerable extent by the report of the 30th annual meeting of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company Limited, which we publish in another column.A concern which employs over7,ooo workmen must of necessity carry considerable weight in the commercial world, and there is no doubt that in the iron and coal trades the doings and progress of the company under notice are watched with interest. We have all heard in recent years, and more frequently during the past twelve months, loud complaints in regard to the state of the iron trade. There is no questioning the fact that it is far from being what it should be. " Short time" has been, of necessity, fre- quently imposed, and the iron-workers have had none too rosy an existance. The iron-masters attribute most of this slackness in trade to the cut-throat price % of the foreigner, and grumble loudly at his" tariff •nurtured competition." Yet, while labouring under such great disadvantages the Pearson and Knowles Company has made a profit on the past year's working of 254,426 18s. 11d., and has distributed in dividends to its 680 shareholders no less than £38,525. The worst that the esteemed chairman, Mr. J. S. Harmood Banner, can say, is that the balance sheet "does not justify us in proposing so large a dividend as last year." The Company has done so well under adverse trading conditions as to earn for its direc. tors and officials the warmest praise and congratu- lation. If they can show such gratifying results at the present time, to what heights of prosperity would the concern rise if the iron and coal trades were in that flourishing condition which all must desire ? Opportunity was taken by the chairman I and his fellow-directors to animadvert on the urgent need for tariff reform. Their arguments have a most convincing ring, but are they not somewhat discounted by the very satisfactory statement of accounts which the Pearson and Knowles Company is able to produce? We doubt not that Mr. Harmood Banner has excellent grounds for saying that "for some

years after the adoption of Mr. Chamberlain's or Mr. Balfour's proposals I should be not indisposed to guarantee to the men of this company more continuous work and higher wages. and to the shareholders better dividends." Probably the chairman of every big trading concern in the country would be able to say the same thing were he at liberty to institute tariffs exactly to his own liking, but this does riot appear a convincing argument in favour of our entering upon a tariff war with every other manufacturing country on the globe. Even Mr. Banner does not guarantee more than a temporary advantage, and a much stronger case requires to be made out before the "Pro:Action bogey' takes the place of that "effete idol called Free Trade."

Mr. Dodds has completed 25 years' service as secretary to the Company and a special resolution of congratulation and thanks was passed in his honour. Mr. G. F. Pearson remarked that" the Company never had a better and more faithful servant than Mr.Dodds,"and other complimentary raferances were made by the Chairman and Mr. J. J. Blackly. An official career of a quarter of a century is no maan record, and we can imagine the pride with which Mr. Dadds will look back upon his connection with the Pearson and Knowles Company, Limited, since its infancy. May we aid our congratulations to those of the directors and shareholders of the Company !

Seven Warrington passive resisters paid the penalty of the law on Tuesday for not paying the Education rate. One was a lady, another a minister,the IA3V. J. F. Matthews, and the remain- ing five were ordinary folk. All proved equally firm in their ddtermination not to pay a rate which they conscientiously objected to, and the usual distress warrants were issued in each case. These have since been acted upon and a sufficiency of goods seized in order to satisfy the rapacious rate- collector. The latest intelligence is that there are 40,000 passive resisters ia the country, and the number is constantly increasing. With the majority of these we have considerable sympathy, but we must dissociate ourselves from those who are prepared to go as far as " E.T.B.' one of the writers for the Protestant Press Agency. This gentleman,presumably one of the cloth—can it be the Rev. E. Theodore Beckett ?—and a preacher of the religion which tells us to love one another, has thrown moderation to the winds in the production of a passive resistance leaflet. He writes in the most sanguinary strain about the "rapacity of hireling priests—black as hell" ; " paltry magistrates clothed with . a little brief authority (very little and very brief) . . . and who do not possess the courtesy of a Hottentot " ; "coxcomb, empty and addle-headed auctioneers, who ought to have been Jeffrey's men' " ; and "the virus of the greatest blasphemy outside hell, Roman Sacerdotalism." So on ad nauseam. Is this passive resistanoe or is it lunacy ? " E.T.8." starts his leaflet with the quotation; "Whom the gods wish to destroy they first drive mad," whieh is unwise of him, for the reader of the leaflet cannot help but fear for the early destruction of the writer, whose mad- ness seems more apparent than that of any- one else. Surely, language such as we have quoted is not going to become the stock-in- trade of the passive resister,? The Protestant Press Agency is taking the swiftest means of alienating all sympathy from those it desires to help. Everybody respects a sacrifice for con- science' sake,but nobody gives more than a passing contemptuous thought to the ranter.

The results of the examination for scholarships tenable at the newly-formed Secondary School together with the report of the inspectors, are published to-day and will no doubt be perused with h interest. That the action of the Education Committee in offering the scholarships has been fully appreciated is evident from the large number of candidates who presented themselves for ' examination. No fewer than 61 students corn- peted for 35 scholarships, and all the more credit is therefore due to those who emerged from the ordeal with flying colours. It is gratifying to be informed that the inspectors regard the results of the examination as showing that the general con- Aition of elementary education in Warringt sn reaches a satisfactory level, and that good material for secondary education can be drawn from the elementary schools. In offering these scholarships the Education Committee is making a praiseworthy effort to encourage the rising generation of the town to enter the teaching pro- fession. Since the new Act came into operation the demand for increased staffs has been so great that in many in stances the authorities have been compelled to go far afield to obtain the services of fully qualified teachers. In the near future it is hoped to obviate this to a great extent, and the committee, we are sure, will have general support iu its efforts to attain so desirable an object.

A great mass of detail was reported to the Education Committee on Monday evening which in itself afforded an evidence of the quiet but solid work which is being done by the sub-committees. It will require the expenditure of much thought and labour before education is thoroughly systematised in Warrington. Hitherto there has been chaotic confusion but there are now indications that something like order and system I have been introduced. That it should have been brought about with such celerity and s 9 little I friction is largely due to the excellent work of the Director of EducaCion. Fortunately,we inive been spared any serious religious bickering arising out of the administration of the new Act, and we hope the managers of the various schools will take a wide and tolerant ontlook in the discharge of their duties. From what transpired at the meeting on Monday there seems some difference of opinion amongst managers as to what proportion of fees shall be refunded to the different schools to meet- certain expenses. It appears to us that the less said about this subject by foundation managers, the better. In these days of professed free elementary education, managers are not likely to assist the finances of the schools by disputing about fees. It might lead to a general demand by parents for free places.

A new difficulty has cropped up in connection with the management of Trust public-houses. It is all very wellito place in charge of an old-estab- lished hostelry an abstemious manager who declines to drink with customers. It is, no doubt, bad for the takings at the house, but then the owners, in their zeal for Gothenburg reform, are prepared to accept a smaller return on capital thsn would have satisfied the former proprietors. Again, a trifling unpleisantness might be caused with the habitues, who may take for churlishness in the manager what sl,ould be recognised at once as superior virtue. Tiles° little troubles, however, might be surmounted; it is when the manager is instructed to discourage drinking, if it goes beyond the limits of legitimate refreshment that the real difficulties begin. Who is to be the judge of excessive drinking when it does not result in drunkenness? A tipsy man may deny the reproach, but his state is evident, if not to himself, at least to those who have not drunk glass for glass with him. But when work- ing men get through 15s. of their wages in beer weekly without so much as shewjng symptoms of looseness at the knee-joints, what is the poor land- lord to do who h4s been instructed to prevent excessive drinking? It is a delicate, not to say dangerous, proceeding to tell a customer who cannot be accined of drunkenness that he has had more liquor than is good for him. Whether such a customer invokes the law for defamation, or adopts a summary method of obtaining redress, the results might be unpleasant, 'and certainly the prospects of future custom would not be groat. The modern publican must in any case be a man of no little tact, for the proper control of a Trust public-house that quality will have to be present in almost statesmanlike proportions.

Some maiming stories are being related about the flotsam and jetsam of society who are turn- ing their attention to politics. The activity of the propagandists and the nearness of a General Election have caused a lot of these people to think that they might earn much needed cash in the work of organisation. Their claims are put for-

ward in eloquent language —so eloquent that one may well doubt whether they would be any use in a bureau. In the old days a good many elections were lost from the sanguine belief of the workers in the value of their canvass, and more especially in platform addresses. The best election agents know that what counts still more is the systematic collection of information as to voters which ensures a well-kept register, and a knowledge of the par- ticular point which appeals most strongly to each elector. When a body of workers is thoroughly organised the people in a constituency are ac- counted for with the same care that a librarian would account for the hooks upon his shelves. They may be closed book ; but there is something on the cover to indicate their character, and they cannot be moved without his knowledge. It is entertaining when one remembers the political dreamer who has failed in shopkeeping thinking that he ought to be given organising work for one or other party. The " Birmingham Daily Post tells of a man in that city who recently demanded a place, on the ground that he was responsible for the passing of the franchise. When he was asked his ground for that assertion he said that for years he had advocated the great concession. "I it was who, in Birmingham Town Hall, seconded a resolution for such a measure."

STACK FIRE AT SUTTON WEAVER. About half-past eight on Monday morning a stack of %tlover belonging to Mr. George Yarwood was fired, it is believed, by a spark from a railwayengine. An alarm was given and the Frodsham Fire Brigade was quickly summoned. Efforts were made to save some portion of the stack, which was valued at .2190, but as there was a very strong wind blowing it was destroyed. The stack was insured.

SICK MAN's STRANGE ESCAPADE.

, Early on Thursday morning James Smith, 12, North Street, Widnes, until recently a Bolton collier, was lying in a state of fever, and when left for a moment by his Wife, who was nursing him, he disappeared. A long search was made by the police for the missing man,who was ultimately discovered at Ditton Junction, about a nine and a half from his home. He was naked witji the exception of a blanket in which he had wrapped him- self. He was in a state of high delirium, and was gently taken home by the police. Later in the morning he was removed to the Widnes Isolation Hospital, the raedical officer attending the case having notified over- night that the man was suffering from typhoid fever.

MAYORALTY OF WIDNES.

On Tuesday afternoon an informal special meeting of the Town Council was held, at which the present Mayor (Alderman W. W. Gossa,ge)was unanimously asked to take the positicpa for a third year. The Mayor thanked the Council, and added that he regretted very much that it was impossible for him to accept their invitation, as he had many important business engagements to fulfil, 'whilst the command to which he had recently been ap- Pointed in connection with the 6th Lancashire Volunteer -Artillery would take a considerable portion of his time. ltwas decided to invite a well-known Widnes alderman Who has not yet passed the chair, to accept office, and a deputation was appointed to waft upon him.

MEMORIAL TABLET AT FRODSHAM. At Frodsham Parish Church on Sunday evening, during divine service, the Vicar (Rev. H. B. Blogg) unveiled the recently erected tablet on the main wall in the Kingsley Chapel, to the memory of the late Mr. John Pollard. The inscription is as follows :—" To the memory of John Pollard, headmaster of the Frodsham Endowed School, 1754-1898. 'I have taught thee in the Way of wisdom, I have led thee in right paths.' This tablet was placed here by his old scholars, as a token of esteem and affectionate regard." The tablet is of fine marble on a background of red granite. The Vicar, in his sermon, referred in the highest terms to the late respected headmaster, who, by his self-sacrificing efforts en behalf of the young, both on Sundays and weekdays, was a worthy pattern and example to his many scholars.

She went to a neighbour's, and defendant then threatened what she would do for her. She retaliated, and her mother, who was taking complain- ant's part, came behind her with a jug, and they were separated.—The case was dismissed. •

AT-';''F7IPTED SUICIDE AT ST. HELENS.

•At St. Helens on Wednesday, John Porter, a teaser, living at 69, Bronte-street, was charged with attempted suicide. Chief Constable Woodstated that the prisoner Worked at Pilkington's Cowley-hill works, and on the 2nd inst. he cut his hand rather badly. He was sent to See a doctor, but instead of that he went home, and his Wife dressed his hand. Soon afterwards he went in the yard and cut his throat. He had been in the Cottage Hospital since. The prisoner said the pain of his hand Was so bad that he did not know what he was doing, and he was sorry for what he had done. He now Premised the magistrates he would look after himself, and he was discharged.

PEARSON AND KNOWLES COAL & IRON CO., LTD. ANNUAL MEETING.

The Iron Trade and Tariff Reform.

The thirtieth annual meeting of shareholders of the above company was held at the company's offices Bewsey-road, Warrington, ou Thursday, September 24th, at 12 o'clock noon, for the purpose of receiving and passing the accounts for the year ending June 30th last, and for transacting the general business of the company. Mr. J. Harmood Banner (chairman of the Board of Directors), presided, and the other directors present were Mr. Wm. H. Bleckly (deputy chairman), Mr. Ed. Beck, Mr. John J. Bleckly, Mr. Geo. F. Pear- son, Mr. Richard Brancker, and Mr. W. Peter Rylands. There were also present the Rev. Philip Hains, Messrs. John Knowles, Charles E. Bunch, David Garnett, W. Richardson, J. Paul Rylands, John Bleckly, jun. A. S. Bleckly, H. J. Bleckly, and A. H. Burgess (share- holders), and Mr. James Dodds (secretary of Ow company). REPORT OF THE DIRECTORS. _

The iepo-r-t—W the directors was as follows:—" The profits for the year ending June 30th, 1903, amount to £53,426 18s. 11d., and the balance brought forward from last year is £7,288 14s. 4d., together £60,715 13s. 3d. An interim dividend at the rate of 6 per cent. per annum on the ordinary shares was paid in March last for the half-year ending December 31st, 1902, amounting to 216,181 ss. The directors now recommend that for the past half-year a dividend at the rate of 6 per cent. per annum on the preference shares and 5 per cent. on the ordinary shares be declared, making a total distribution for the year of £38,525. The directors further recom- mend that £lO,OOO be written off the colliery leases and goodwill account, reducing this item to £100,000; and £5,000 be added to the reserve fund, making the total £200,000; and that the balance of £7,190 13s. 3d. be carried forward. The business of the company dur- ing the year has suffered, both in coal and iron, from slackness of demand and declining prices. In compli- ance with the articles of association, Mr. J. S. Harmood Banner retires from the Board, and is eligible for re- election. The auditors, Messrs. Bless° and Sons, also retirMand are eligible for re-election.—(Signed) J. S. In accordance with the provisions of the Companies Act, 1900, we certilly that all our requirements as- Auditors have been complied with. We report to the Shareholders that we have audited the above Balance Sheet, and in our opinion such Balance Sheet is properly drawn up so as to exhibit a true and correct view of the statelof the Company's affairs as shown by the books. There is a liability on Bills receivable discounted„and also in connection with certain Shares.

BLEASE & SONS, Auditors. Chartered Accountants, Warrington, 3rd September, 1903. CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH.

The CHAIRMAN, in moving "That the reports and accounts now submitted be approved and adopted," said :—I will, as usual, refer to the figures in the balance sheet, which, whilst they do not justify us in proposing so large a dividend as last year, are very satis- factory,as showing the liquid position of our assets and justifying the high position of credit which the com- pany maintains. We have no debenture debt or charge upon the company's resources of any kind; and, whilst you will see on the'credit side of our account that our liabilities are larger than last year, yet it is only the ordinary incidence of the time when accounts fall due, and there is nothing exceptional about it. On the debit Bich! of the account the cash and bills stand at R 88,999, against £64,309 last year. The debtors stand at £103,037 against £36,954, both improved positions. The stock, on the other band, is less: £225,893 against £233,611 last year, and this is accounted for partly by the reduced price at which stocks are taken, and PartlY by reduction in stock in the iron department. It may. interest you to know, as showing the care with which we take stock, that our dead buffer waggons, of which we hold about 1,000, are esti- mated, for the purpose of this balance sheet, at £5 per waggon, within a price at which we could realize them, though we hope to use them for many years to come. The principal item on the balance sheet—property, Sce. —is, you will see, larger by £6,251 than this time last year, the figures being :-1903, £560,811; 1902, £554,560. This amount represents actual capital expenditure On colliery account, £9,4164 iron works, £8,546 less reduc- tion for removals or destruction of old plant, receipts from sales of land and coal property, and an amount of depreciation which, in addition to the amount we show ,on the balance sheet, we annually write off our coal property against the waste caused by its exhaustion. The two large items which remain are :—On the debit side, the goodwill, £110,000; and on the credit side, the reserve, £195,000. You will have seen in the report how, subject to your approval, we propose to demi with these two accounts, which will, after the transfers have been made, stand at: —Credit, reserve, £200,000; debtor, goodwill, £lOO,OOO. As with the exception of £60,000 we have not our reserve fund specially invested, I think 'it not unlikely that, in the next accounts th directors submit, they will extinguish the goodwill by transfer from the reserve of £lOO,OOO, and leave the amount at credit of the reserve of £lOO,OOO ; not that the goodwill account is nota valuableassetas represent- ing out trade-marks, patterns, agencies, all the other attachments to trade which grow up with an old, well- established business, and not that we should dispose of it except at a considerable figure, but we have been in existence 29 years, and though we paid £315,559 on the foundation of the company for goodwill we are pleased to see it written off our accounts, and are glad the time has come when we are able to do it. Now, you may well inquire what 'we do with this, money which we reserve out of our profits for depreciation of plant and goodwill; and, though I have explained it at pre- vious meetings, I will touch upon it again, as it de- serves repeticion to impress on shareholders the abso- lute necessity of their directors using every effort to keep their company in the front rank as regards every facility of trade and every improvement of manufac- ture in order to ensure cheap and good workmanship. In the ironworks they have considerably improved the machinery of some of the means of manufacture, as I could shew you if any of you cared to go over the yard after the meeting. We have erected a large covering on the shed in which the enginbering department is carried on. We have to adopt, in the engineering department, a part of the work to electricity; and in connection with waggon wheels, of which we are large makers, we shall have this year to make alterations in our plant to meet the demands of the railway companies. No doubt you will have seen the discussions of railway methods In England and America, particularly with regard to

===== 0002601_19030926_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
TELE SIZE OF RAILWAY WAGGONS;

and, as you know, the English railways are to I a considerable extent, adopting a stronger and more capacious waggon, and are, in consequence, endeavouring to insist upon private traders doing the same. No doubt it is bound to come, and serious as it is to private traders and to ourselves as colliery proprietors, owning over 2,000 waggons, of which, however, not more than one-half are of the old type; it is also serious to our iron works as 'necessitat- ing a complete new plant to enable us to make the wrought iron wheels instead of as hitherto cast iron wheels. We must, however, be ready to meet the wants of our customers. Mr. Herbert Bleckly, head of the engineering department, has been giving great attention to this matter and has made a point of discussing it thoroughly with the railway companies so that we shall not be behindhand in our knowledge. We hope to be quite up to date. I should like to acknowledge the courtesy and the fairness which the officials of the rail- ways have extended to us—not only our near neigh- bours, the L. and N.-W. Railway and the L. and Y. Rail- way, but also the railways further off—in opening their minds to us and showing us what they had best found to meet their owianecessities. That addice and assistance had been of the greatest service to us, though we have notaltogether seen eye to eye; our view has been that a 15-ton waggon was ?the best adapted to our colliery requirements, whilst their view is towards a larger size. We have ordered 100 waggons of 15-ton capacity, and some are already delivered and working well, and I hope the railway companies will see that not only they but we obtain in reduction of rates some of the advantages which the lessened haulage will afford. last years, accounted for by the continual drop in the price of conl during the year. The profits of each de- partment are about equally distributed and fairly repre- sent the relative amounts of capital employed. In the coal trade we had hoped for a reduction of cost owing to reduced rate of wages, but the submission to Lord James resulted in his refusal of the application by the coalowners, and the rate of wages has remained at the same figure, notwithstanding the fall in the price of coal. The subject will no doubt come up again, and, whilst we all should rejoice with the coal workers if such an improvement in trade resulted as necessitated no reduction, yet ,Ae think the miners ought willingly , to meet the present depression, which they must acknowledge. The profits of the iron trade have im- proved during ale past year, and the directors are glad to say that they have found the arrangement referred to last year for combination with a firm of similar out- put of the greatest service, and it has assisted largely in the regularity of their production, a matter of the greatest moment for economical working. Of the future, it is difficult to say anything sanguine. We are well equipped to meet any demand and to keep cost of manufacture down, but the intention of the Steel Trust of America to fight the depression in trade there by finding a dumping ground hare, which, with OITR PRESENT FISCAL ARRANGEMENTS, we cannot fightagainst, must injuriously affect the iron trade and consequently coal. Let us hope .American trade will soon revive. It is not often the duty of a chairman of a limited company to refer to any matter , many way bearing on politics, but the interests of the iron and coal trades are so bound up with the present . . .

was steel, and only 58 per cent was iron. Moreover in 1892-3 every ton of material we used was of British *manufacture, whereas last year we paid £60,000 for raw material from abroad. The alterations here indicated relate to our manufaCturing, changes, and in regard to our customers the position; is equally striking. Ten years ago 53 per cent represented our home trade and 47 per cent our export trade to our Colonies, and to foreign markets; last year our export trade was only 24 per cant, and our home trade was 76 per cent. This might be quite satisfactory if our home trade repre- sented new business, as it is for many reasons better to supply the man across the street than the man across the ocean. But this home trade is not new trade—it has been wrested from competitors arnongst English iron makers less able to stand the brunt of the battle than ourselves and less furnished with ample capital resources that we have at command, and it may be less competent for their work than we are. Many of them have fallen out by the way, and their works are closed. I think these facts and figures justify me in saying that unless we are vigilant, alert and enterprising in the management of our business and ready to change with the times as we have shown ourselves to be in the past, we cannot maintain in the future the position our company has won. I have no wish to enter the polemical arena opened up by the Chairman' nor to go over the pros and cons of fiscal reform or the discussion of those questions which are at present shaking the very foundations of politi- cal life, nor do I pass any judgment as to whether our trade is really free or fettered under existing con- ditions ; but what I have described is not without its _

fiscal question, brought forward by Sir. Chamberlain and Mr. Balfour, from their different views, that, not as a political question, but as a matter of the greatest importance to the iron trade, I feel bound to refer to it andto ask the assistance of our workmen and share- holders with their influence and suppott. We have 7,150 workmen and 680 shareholders in our company, so that there is a good strong moral force to appeal to, and we trust they will see eye to eye with us and realise their position as individual members of a great trading company with power to aid in its welfare. We, as a nation, need some review of our fiscal position—and largerhome markets—the best markets of all—in the shape of attached and mutual-aiding communities.' The United States of America and United Germany possess andpre,serve the home markets in their vast territories and we ought, with our Colonies and Intlia, to follow; their example. There has been a good deal of prophesy- ing lately to the effect that the adoption of Mr. Chamberlain's and Mr. Balfour's proposal would mean DEARER FOOD to the people of this country. It is easy to prophesy. I would go further, and say that for some years after the adoption of Mr Caomberlain's or Mr. Balfour's pro- posals I should be not indisposed to guarantee to the men of this company more continuous work and higher wages, and to the shareholders better dividends, (Applause). significance in relation to such matters, and if our ex- perience is borne out by the experience of other trades —and British manufacturers are becoming continually more and more dependent upon foreign sources for the supply of their raw material, and at the same time losing ground in foreign markets by the competition of bounty-supported manufacturers whose home markets enable them to sell elsewhere below cost—it may well be that fiscal arrangements which suited well enough the trade of 20 to 40 years ago are unsuitable, and re- quire amendment before they will fit equally well the conditions of trade as it is to-day. PROTECTION AND THE IRON TRADE.

Letter from Alderman Monks.

Alderman Monks writes as follows to the Editor of the" Manchester Guardian."

Sir,—Your correspondent "T. 8. while professing to reply to my letter, really deals with an entirely differ- ent phase of the question. I fail to see how a trans- ference of works to the coast, is going to help any iron manufacturer to keep his home trade. Those works situ ated inland are near the raw materials(pig iron =a coal), and equally near their customers. Notwithstanding these natural advantages the foreigner is offering iron at lower prices than such iron would cost to pioducq at English works, even those whose proprietors have spent large sums in remodelling and reorganising their plant.

Were it not for their protective duties the foreigners could not sell their iron at the "cut-throat prices" that have obtained during the last two years, and it is just this pointl desire to bring out and emphasise, be- cause it seems to me so unreasonable and so unfair.

How can any industry withstand such competition RS this with any chance of success? If your correspond- ent will suggest how this difficulty is to be met he will confer an nndonbted benefit upon the iron trade as a whole.—Yours, F. W. MONKS. Warrington, September 21.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFEBOAT SATURDAY. The Mayor and the Recent Demonstration.

In a letter addressed to Mr. Stanley Wilson, the hon_ secretary of the Warrington Lifeboat Saturday Corn mittee, the Mayor expresses his hearty acknowledg- ment and sincere thanks to Mr. Wilson and through him to all those who worked so well, so long, and so energetically to render the demonstration an unqualified success. "Mrs. Parr, as President of the Ladies' Com- mittee" continues the Mayor "joins me in sincere congratulations and grateful thanks to all the willing and enthusiastic workers •to whom our noble institution is indebted for the magnificent sum of upwards of £350 which, I am told has been realized by their exertions. It would be difli- cult to particularise where all have done so well, but your name and Mr. Davies's must head the list, which would be too long if even half the other deserving names had to be included. We have every reason to be proud of the way Warrington has recognized the efforts of this deserving and most worthy institution, and to feel that the calls of charity and deserving need have only to be made known to command the sympathy and ensure the help of all, from the highest to the humblest. I am sure all concerned must feel very happy that their united efforts have been crowned with such signal suc- cess."

THE TOTAL PROCEEDS. Award of Collecting Prizes.

At a meeting of the Finance Committee held last evening, under the presidency of Mr. J. H. Smethurst. it was reported that the gross receipts to date amounted to £397 18s. 411 The number of coins counted was estimated at .66,921, weighing 7651b5., or 6cwts. 3qrs. Silver lifeboat pendants were awarded as follows for best hotel collections :—Norton Arms Hotel, amount collected, £2 10s. 51d. ; Midland Hotel; £2 3s. ; king and Queen Hotel; £1 19s. 3d.; Cemetery Hotel, £1 165,; Talbot Inn, £1 2s 511 Other awards were made as follows :

Gentleman with the largest box collection : Mr Frank White (Sunny Jim), Lovely-lane, who collected £3 2s. 101 d. ; prize, writing cabinet. Lady with largest box collection, Miss Crowley (Eastern Dancing Girl), Liverpool, who collected £3 7s. ; prize, cut glass and silver cake dish. Gentleman with largest bag collection, Mr. Joseph Davies, 2, Brookland-street, Warrington, who collected £2 is. 21d. ; prize, silver and amber cigar holder. Lady with largest bag collection, Miss Knight, Hewitt-street, Latchford, who collected £2 10s. 94d., prize, silver-mounted oak salt cellars. Miss Knight was also awardeda special prize of a silver boat pendant.

A fancy dress ball in aid of the Lifeboat Institution will be held in Parr Hall, on Thursday October Bth. STOCKTON HEATH.

EDUCATION.—Mr. E. Bradshaw, clerk to the Parish Council, has received from the Board of Education a draft final order made under the new Education Act setting forth the regulations which shall govern the appointment of foundation managers of St. Thomas's National School. The usual clauses as to qualifications are inserted. ST. TIIO.MAS' MEN'S CLASS.--In connection with this class an enjoyable !outing to Southport took place on Saturday afternoon. The Rev. C. AiMarkharn Evans and Mr. H. J. Westbrook accompanied the party.• I LACK OF ENTHUSIASM.—A meeting to further con- sider the proposal to form a Stockton Heath Cricket Club was held at the National Schools on Tuesday evening. The Vicar (Rev. H. V. Pigot) presided over a small attendance, including Messrs. Welsby, Ruddock, Mclntyre, Oliff(secretary),and the Rev. C. A. M. Evans. It was stated that a suitable field had been secured,and from a discussion it appeared that the cost of laying out the ground, rent, &c., would be about £3O. Several expressed disappointment that so few were present,and remarked they did not think it would be advisable to proceed any further with the matter, as so little enthusiasm seemed to be displayed. Eventually a motion was passed to the effect that, "in consequence of the poor response to the'appeal sent out, no steps be taken." The matter was, therefore, adjourned sine die. Mr. Pigo.t was thanked for presiding.

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

SEVEN GASES HEARD AT WARRINGTON. A Minister's Objections.

Passive resistance has taken practical shape in War- rington and on Tuesday seven resisters were summoned to appear before the magistrates at the Borough police court for non-payment of the rate for education purposes. Great interest was take lt in the proceedings and there was a large attendance df the public. The magistrates on the bench were Dr. Fox (in the chair) and Mr. J. Rymer Young. _ _ _

-The first cis' e taken was that of the Rev. J. F. Matthews, Baptist minister, Latch ford,who has declared his willingness to go to prison rather than pay the rate. He admitted owing ss. The Chairman: Why don't you payi? Defendant: I willingly acknowledge the legality of the rate—

The Chairman Very well, we are going on legal grounds. Defendant: I know that, but I refuse to pay for the propagation of Roman Catholicism. _

- the-Chairman (warmly): We have nothing to do with that, sir. .You are legally bound to pay, and we make the order.

Defendant: I understand that, and I submit; but I ask your Worships to kindly listen to my statement. I think I am entitled by the terms of the summons to state my objectron even though your Worships might overrule the objections as not being distinctly legal, and I ask you to courteously allow me to state the nature of my objection. The Chairman: We have only the legal question to face and nothing else, and I decline to enter into any- thing else. 1 Defendant: I can only state what I have said before that I refuse to pay, and shall refuse to pay, to support Roman Catholicism and Anglicanism in any shape- The Chairman: We have nothing to do with that and I decline to enter into it.

Defendant: That is the whole of my objection, and I shall put it entirely beyond your Worships' power next time for you even to issue a distress warrant. I am much obliged to you for having listened so courteously. to what I have said. _ _ _

Mr. Matthews was loudlyapplauded on concluding his remarks.

There was another outburst of applause when the name of Miss Emma J. Fieldhouse, Bridge-street, was called.

The Chief Constable: I shall have to ask your Worships for an order to clear the court if this goes on. The Chairman: Certainly. Miss Fieldhouse admitted owing .10s. 7d. but said sh,‘ was not willing to pay it. The magistrates granted the usual distress warrant. Mr. William Clatworthy, 21, Lovely-lane, secretary of the Warrington Citizens' League, was summoned in respect of an unpaid rate of 6s. Rd. Mr. Clatworthy admitted the rate and refused to pay for similar reasons to those stated by thousands of his fellow countrymen. He did not think he need enlarge upon it.

'The Chairman: Certainly not. Continuing Mr. Clatworthy willingly admitted the ~gality of the rate, but denied the morality of it. The Chairman : Then we make the order.

Mr. Nilliam Gallop, 69, Butterm—a-rice-t---sir—oet,admitted owing 10s. and said he objected to the way education was ruled.

The• Chairman : Don't you know it is a legal debt? Mr. Gallop: It is legal as far as the law goos but it is not justice. The Chairman: That is all we want. Order made.

_ Mr. Norman Balmford, Sankey-street, President of the Citizens' League, was summoned for non-payment of a rate of £1 3s. 9d. He advanced similar objections to those already raised. The Chairman: You admit it is a legal claim? Mr. Balmford: Yes.

The Chairman: Order granted. Mr. Edward. Howard, 192, Padgate-lane, admitted owing 3s. 6d.

The. Chairman: Why don't you pay? Mr. Howard: I feel I cannot violate my conscience by paying the rate voluntarily. As Mr. Howard admitted the legality of the claim distress warrant was issued.

William Meyer, 33, Froghall-lane, was summoned in respect of 6s. 10s. He did not appear and a distress warrant was issued.

Mr. Matthews, at the close, said he should like to be allowed to publicly thank, on behalf of his colleagues and himself, the assistant overseer (Mr. W. H. Wood- cock) for the courtesy and consideration with which he had met them at every point in this unfortunate and unpleasant business. He asked the magistrates to grant his application to have the distress warrants issued on one order to minimise expenses. The Magistrates' Clerk (Mr. G. P. P. White): There is no objection. All right, Mr. Matthews, they will all be on one.

Mr. Matthews: lam very much obliged to your Worships.

EXECUTING THE WARRANTS. During the afternoon Police Sergeants Cheney and Smith levied distress on the goods of the passive resis- ters against whom orders had been made. One distress warrant sufficed for all the cases.

The officers first visited the house of the Rev. J. F. Matthews, at Latchford, and were cordially received by the reverend gentleman, who handed over a chippendale ' table. This was promptly conveyed on the tram to the Borough Treasurer's office. The officers proceeded in a cab to the homes of the six other resisters. Miss Fieldhouse handed over a travelling clock, and a small marble clock was obtained from Mr. Howard. Messrs. Gallop and Balmford parted with watches and chains, Mr. Meyer with a pair of bronze ornaments, and Mr. Clatworthy with a pair of small silver candlesticks SUMMONSES AT WrDNES. Before the Mayor (Alderman W. W. Gossage) and Alderman Wareing, at the Widnes Police Court, on Mon- day, Mr. T. Gillbody, a.seistaut overseer for the town- ship of Hale, applied for summonses against William Knowles, Halebank ; Alexander Cameron Mitchell, Hale- wood; and W J. Sell, Halewood, for non-payment of that part of the poor rate set aside for educational purposes. The applications were granted. NEWTON CASES. • The Rev. J. H. Riddette, Congregational Church, Hay- dock, was summoned at Newton-le-Willows on Thursday He7declined to pay three-fifths ,of ' the amount due (2s 6d) because he would be paying for the spread of a religion which was contrary to the dictates of his con- science._ Aniorder_was made.

John Percival, of Crow lane, Newton-le-Willows, sent his wife to appear for him. The bench declined to hear her, and adjourned the case for a fortnight.

SCANT COURTESY FOR PASSIVE RESISTERS. Nine passive resisters at Chester City Police Court on Thursday were dealt with in a summary manner, which contrasted with the indulgence accorded thirty- nine others a 2fortnight [ago. The Bench refused to listen to any conscientious _objections, and as none had legal objections the cases were quickly disposed of Some laughter was caused and the chairman threatened to clear the court. One defendant who lingered in the box with a view of stating his moral objections was sharply told to go. The usual orders were made. •

CASES AT MANCHESTER. MR. EDWYN HOLT A DEFENDANT.

The first batch of " passive resisters "appeared before Mr. E. Brierley, second stipendiary, and other magis- trates, at the Manchester city Police Court, on Wed- nesday in answer to summonses for the non-payment of theif poor-rate. The defendants were the Rev. Dr, Leach, pastor of Cavendish Chapel, and chairman of the Passive Resistance League; the Nev. G. E. Roberts, pastor of Union Chapel, Oxford-street; the Rev. C. H. Watkins, pastor of Coupland-street Baptist Chapel ; Mr. Edwyn Holt, formerly chairman of the Watch Com- mittee ; Mr. R. D. Darbishire, one of the Whitworth Trustees and an honorary freeman of the city of Man- chester; Miss Hadfield, sister of Mr. George Hadfieltd, one of the city magistrates; and the Rev. C. W. Macal- pin.

The first case disposed of was that of Mr. R. D Darbishire.7-Ifr. Darbishire said Manchester was his birthplace; he had passed his youth in the city, his office was there, and the city had been his field of work, and had teen too good to him in many ways. It was therefore with regret that he had to withhold the municipal rate amounting to LI 18s. 2d. He had been obliged to do so because what had been called an educa- tion rate had been put in a joint claim. He believed both were illegitimately demanded without a separate statement and separate authority. Another point was that he disclaimed any obligation to this enactment. Mr. Brierley.—There will be a distress warrant. Mr. Edwyn Holt was next afforded an opportunity of stating his objection to the rate. He said he would say at once that his refusal to pay the rate was not on political grounds ; it was solely on conscientious grounds. Mr. Brierley.—l accept that; but it is no defence. • Mr.. Holt.—l would like you to know,sir,that I cannot voluntarily pay for the teaching of doctrines which I believe to be harmful. (Loud applause in court.) Mr. 13rierley said he took that for granted, and he must ask Mr. Holt to confine himself to some legal objection. Mr. Holt went on to say that' the Act taught children to forsake the faith of their fathers, and despise the religion of their homes. As a good citizen he did not want to break the law, but he could not break his conscience. (Renewed applause.) Mr. Brierley said there would be a warrant in this case also.

The Rev. Dr. Leach, in an impassioned address to the court, said he objected to being summoned in respect of the poor rate, which he had already paid. As a respectable citizen it was hardly fair that he should be so summoned. (Applause.) He said he could con- ceive it possible that by brute force they could get a law, which would destroy their homes, auction their churches, and banish them from fatherland, but they could never make the people pay this wicked rate. (Applause.)

A warrant was grantei for distraint in default of pay ment and warrants were granted in other cases. A demonstration Was subsequently organised by the local Passive Resistance League.

ANONYMOUS SETTLEMENTS.

Eighty persons Irti been sum noned to appear before the Ipswich magistrates on Friday for non-payment of the education rate, the defendants including Mr. Ford Goddard, Liberal member for the borough. and other prominent passive resisters.Onffednesday morning,how- ever; the superintendent rate collector found in his letter-box 28 sovpreigns, with an anonymous type-written communication stating the money was for the payment of the rates in question and the cost of the summonses.. The amount being just sufficient for this purpose, legal proceedings will be abandoned.

BARRISTER INSISTS ON GOING TO PRISON,

Councillor D. Warde, a barrister, of Richmond, has says a local correspondent, vested the whole of his property in trustees, and absolntely refuses to pay the education rate (2s. by distraint or money. He states that it will be the proudest day in his life when he Is sent to prison for this amount.-

Passive Resistance.

connection with the recent Court proceed- ings, I should like, through the medium of your columns, to tell the public that the quietness and order that prevailed, after the first two outbursts of applause, during the remainder of the proceedings, were entirely • due to the tactful courtesy in our Chief Constable'sr,request for silence to the sympathizing public present, and certainly not to the harsh, cart address of the presiding magistrate Dr. Fox. Some of these gentlemen seem to need reminding that they are men administering the law to men. We don't come to this business as criminals. We come as honourable citizens, bringing an intelligence equal to that which has to receive us and we demand a citizen's respect. May I also add, to avoid any misconception in the minds of those who would otherwise wonder at a Nonconformist selling our goods, that in consenting to conduct that sale Mr. W. Hickman has done so at the express wish of the resisters thsmselves. This assurance, we trust, will avoid the risk of any hostile demonstration, at least to him. The sale must take place and it -is, perhaps, the best to be in friendly hands.—Your t faithfully, WILLIAM CLATWORTHI% Secretary and Treasurer, Warrington Passive Resistance League.

MR. MATTHEWS AND HIS EXPERI- ENCES AS A RESISTER.

Dr. Fox's Attitude.

INTERESTING INTERVIEW.

A reporter of the "Examiner" waited upon. the Rev. J. F. Matthews, minister of Latchford Baptist Chapel, yesterday, to obtain an expression of opinion from him on his experiences as a passive resister. Mr. Matthews was engaged in some private tutorial work at the time, but with his usual courtesy he de- voted a few minutes to comply with the reporter's request. In answer to a preliminary question as to his opinion on the conduct of the Bench at the Police Court on Tuesday, Mr. Matthews said

I was a little disappointed that . Dr. Fox, in his capacity of Chairman of the Bench, was not a little more courteous in his treatment of passive re- sisters. You see the woman who immediately pre- ceded the passive resisters was allowed by the Chair- man to state the reasons why her husband had not paid the rate. It was not a legal objection which she raised, but as soon as I made an objection which was not on legal grounds he would not hear anything but what was legal. That was the only thing I thought was a little unfair on the part or Dr. Fox. Still the Court listened to what I had to say and that was all I cared about."

" And how did the police officers carry out the distress warrant ?" queried the rep3rter. "The execution of the warrant on the part of Sergeants Cheney and Smith was carried out with the most becoming courtesy. I don't think any police officers in the kingdom could have done the thing more courteously. They came into the house, and instead of laying hands on things said : Nratthews what- would you like us to take?' I told them the place was before them, and they could take anything they pleased. I asked them if they cared to take the marble timepiece on the mantelpiece, but they thought that too valuable, and said they simply wanted to take something that would cover the necessary expenses. If the assistant overseers and po!ice offi,.-ers through- out the country had acted like our Warrington officials there would not have been half as mach heart-burning, and yet it Would have allowed the passive resisters to make the protests which by right they are com- pelled to make. I also think the passive • resisters ow-ea great debt of gratitude to Mr. George White, the magistrates' clerk, for his action in meeting the passive resisters in the way he did by making one warrant suffice for all the passive resisters." "The sale of distraine,d goods,l suppose, will take place next week"? "Yes, on Tuesday next at 2.3oo'clock in the Market Place. Mr. William Hickman, I understand, will sell the goods without charging any fee or expenses. All round we have great reason to be satisfied with the way things have gone on in Warrington. My only hope is that there Will be no rowdyism. at the sale because as a passive resister I object to anything which may bring discredit upon any public protest one may have to make."

" Have any arrangements been• made to buy in the goods ? " "I am making no arrangements for that. goods go so far as lam conerted." • "There will be another rate to pay six months hence, what do you intend to do thou? " I feel after all, that this distraint is only another way of our paying the rate. I object to pay this edu- cation rate, either compulsorily or voluntarily. I don't intend to allow it to be done compulsorily. I would much rather suffer what alternative penalty the law can impose. It is not bemuse I want to gain a little notoriety. Far from it. I should be content, it I had to make any reputation at all that the reputa- tion should come through sincere and earnest work as a minister of Jesus ehrist. The publicity which has necessarily come through these passive resistance proceedings is to me in some of its aspects most objectionable. In my case, before I knew any- thing of any formal passive resistance in the country, as soon as that; fateful clause 7 was dropped out of the Education Act I made up my mind that I should pay no rate, whatever anyone else might do. I was certainly the first one to speak publicly on this Educa- tion Act in. Warrington, and the " Examiner " was good enough to give an extensive report at the time of what I said."

The

"Has the publicity of the passive resistance pro- ceedings brought you any correspondence?" " Yes. I have had a note from a man who was dis- trained a little while ago in Penrith asking me for certain information. I have also had an - invitation from another quarter to speak on the passive resist- ance movement." *-

STOCKTON HEATH'S REPRESENTATION. . -- Decision of the Rural Council Sub-Committee.

A meeting of the Sub-committee of the Runcorn Rural District Council appointed to re-consider the application of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee for additional representation on the Council was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. There were present:— Messrs. G. Pilling (in the chair), Joseph Knowles, Joseph Alounfield, S. Woodward. and G. F. Ashton. (clerk). Mr. James Whitley and Mr. Edwin Bradshaw attended to state the case for Stockton Heath. They pointed out that within the past five years the popula- tion and the ratable value of the parish had very materially increased, the present population being over 3,000. Stockton Heath was the third largest district in the Union as regarded ratable value and the second as regarded population. A great many houses were at present in course of erection new streets were being formed, and the Corporation contemplated extending the tramway to the village. The County Council had already granted additional Parish Councillors, and it was contended that the cir- cumstances warranted the granting of another repre- sentative on the District Council.

After a sh-or-Cdrsc—us—slo-n—,-th-e—ommittee decided to recommend the District Council to make application to the County Council for another District Councillor for Stockton Heath.

It is probable that if the Committee's decision receives the early approval of the District and County Conncils,the election of another repmsentative will take, place about March next,

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Laza: Applications at the Consistory Court.

The Recent Fire at Newchurch.

A consistory court was held on Tuesday at the Church House,Lord-street,Liverpool,Chancellor Espin presiding. The Rev. E. W. W. Kaye,rector of Newchurch,Calcheth, near Warrington, applied for a faculty authorising the erection of a new church on the site of the building destroyed by fire in April last. Plans, which had been approved by the vestry—whose representattve was Captain Within.gton, Culc,heth Hall—were submitted, and it was state 4 that the total cost of the reconstruc- tion iwould be £7,300,0f which a littlepver £6,000 was in hand. The new church would provide additional accommodation for 100 persons. The faculty was decreed in the terms of the citation, the Chancellor re- marking that the new building would be much more ornate and suitable than the former one, and he wished the rector and wardens every success in their work. An application was made by the Rev. H. L. Wadeson, rector of Croft, near Warrington, for permission to remove the pulpit, the Whole of the seats, to take up and remove the floor and replace it with wooden blocks or cement, and also to erect a vestry for the choir, and to provide an apparatus for the heating of the church. The total:cost of the work was to be also, all of which sum was in hand. The faculty was decreed in the terms f the citation.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. There Was a 'large congregation in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon at the open mooting of the P.S.A. Society. The chair was taken by Mr. A. Corney and an address was delivered by Mr. Walley, of Manchester. In the course of his remarks Mr. Walley impressed upon his hearers the 'important part, they played in life and the influence they had upon others. Everyone was a sower, either for good or evil, and the seed we sowed would certainly bear fruit.—Mr. W. H. Wallington and Mr. Arthur Griffiths sang as a duet "Jesus Lover of my Soul." Mr. Griffiths and Mrs. Arthur Booth after- wards sang together in the duet "Love Divine." Mrs. Booth also sang the difficult solo "I will extol thee." All the vocalists sang with taste and expression and their singing was greatly appreciated. Mr. Payton pre- sided at the organ. The Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) announced a further increase of membership, the num- bers now exceeding 500. It was decided to senda letter of sympathy to the relatives of a deceased member, Mr. Ileald.—Mr. W. Beswick offered prayer and the dhair- man read the scripture lesson.

Baptist Church, Colborne Street. At this church on Sunday, Mr. James Bailey preached in the morning, while the evening service was taken by Mr. Edward Jones; the subject of • his sermon being "Moral] and spiritual growth." Growth was one of the most interesting things in nature, the preacher stated, and the human soul was intended for an in- definite growth, being as a writer had once said like "An imiil9rtal acorn in whose shell boundless forests sleep." Ml.ny interesting comparisons were drawn from nature, but, unlike nature, the more a soul received the more its capacities widened, spiritual supplies only in- tensifying the mind's desire and increasing its yearn- ing for more, and its soil grew richer in proportion to the fruit it yielded. To grow in goodness, however,was no easy matter, and it needed the noblest man or woman to be a Christian in the highest and truest sense. Conversion from sin was necessary, but this should be followed by spiritual growth, for which the means were communion with God and our own activity, for moral and spiritual power, just like the muscles of the body, depended upon exercise, and they ought, there- fore, to work earnestly in the Master's service. There , were glorious possibilities in the Christian life, and he I urged men to look to the Christ to enable them to grove in all the elements of moral strength and moral splen- dour, and to go on from strength to strength in every- thing that was pure and lovely and divine.

Thirtieth Anniversary of Wycliffe Church.

Sunday last was the 30th-auniversar-y of the opening of Wycliffe Church, Bewsey-street, and the services were made special for the occasion. The Re'. , John Yonge preached morning and evening to fairly large congre- gations. He based his morning discourse on the text "Awake, awake; put on bhystrength, 0 Zion; put on thy beautiful garments 0 Jerusalem, the holy city" (Isaiah 52 c., 1 v). The call of the prophet,he said, had a present day meaning, applicable to the Christian Church of everyname. He felt, that it was peculiarly a,pplicable on that anniversary as a call to greater Christian activity. The Church of Christ at the beginning of this 20th century was only partially awakened •to the glory of her destiny and the greatness of her mission. There was a prevalent powerlessness and consequent depression and despondency. Every day almost, one heard the libel repeated that Christianity was played out and sometimes it certainly did look as if Christians believed it to be true. But he believed there was an awakening in every church in Christendom and we were rapidly approaching the dawn of a Letter day of greater usefulness and richer power for the Church of Christ. Such ought to be their most importunate prayer. An anniversary day was not altogether a day for reviewing the past. It could be batter utilised to encouragea new start towards a great and better future. They ought never as Christian people, to sink down into a hopeless pessimism or give way to murmurings that former days were better than the present. Rather ought it to become customary with them to believe and endeavour that always in some highest and noblest sense, the future was going to be better than the past. They must stimu- late themselves to energies and new enterprises. The preacher proceeded to affirm that the supreme end and destiny of the church was to redeem men from sin, selfishness, temporality and worldliness, and appealed to all connected with Wycliffe to make such supreme aims and endeavours paramount.—The choir sang the anthem "Oh sin.' unto the Lord," the solo being efficiently taken by Mr. Arthur Griffiths.—At the evening service two anthems were sung. Mr. Griffiths sang the solo part in "Oh come let us worship" and Mrs. Pearson gave an effective rendering of the solo passages in " Hear my pntyer " (Mendelssohn). Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. presided at the organ. The collections amounted to £2O The Ex-Presittent of the Conference at Warrington. For some years past it has been the oustom to invite each successive ex-president of the WesfeyanConference to preach the anniversary services in connection with Bold-street Wesleyan Church. Dr. Shaw Banks, of Headingly College, Leeds, completed his term of office as President of the Conference in July last, and the Wesleya,ns of Warrington were offered an opportunity of hearing him on Shriday. In the morning Dr. Banks preached a sermon from the words lam the vine, ye are the branches, he that abideth in me and I in him.the same will bear much fruit." The preacher contended that there must of necessity be a close affinity between the Lord Jesus Christ and the Christian, as exampled in the branches of the vine tree to the trunk, and Christ's follower must be permeated with the godly, gentle, and meek spirit of the Saviour for him to bear fruit in a holy, godly, life. The "Te Deum" was sung to a special setting, whilst the choir also rendered "The Wilderness" (Goss), Mr. E. Pearce giving a remarkably fine interpretation of the solo item. In the evening Dr. Shaw Banks preached to a large congregation. He based his discourse on the words "I follow after, if that I may apprehend that for which also I am apprehended of Christ Jesus" (Philip- pians 3c. 12v.). The first thing they saw in Paul's con- version, and every conversion,said the preacher, was the large place which Divine power:played in it. All through scripture salvation was represented as God's gift to man. In speaking of the change ' which conversion brought about, the preacher said there could not be stronger proof of the genuiness of conversion than the way in which it enabled a man to deny all his former habits and ways, and make God's will the lawand guide of all his conduct. God's own description of it was "1 will give you a new heart; I will put a new spirit within you." In every conversion, as in St. Paul, God had a purpose in view. There was a plan—a divine plan—for everyone of us. Conversion did not mean merely our own salvation. There was something beyond that. It was that the message might go through us to others, that they might be His witnesses, not simply by word or by spoken testimony butby living experience. We took too narrow a view of salvation and what it meant and the change that it made if we thought of it as bearing only upon our own safety and our own peace. Every Christian was to be a channel through which the same grace and loveand peace and power came to others. —Special music was rendered by the choir under tiae con- ductorship of Mr. F. Hickman. who presided at the organ. An effective rendering was given of theanthems "Oh gladsome light" and "0 Father whose almighty power." Mr. Levi Riohawdson's tenor voice was heard to advantage in the solo "There is a green hill." Col- lections on behalf of the trust fund realised about £l7.

Hill Cliffe Baptist Chapel.

There were large congregations at the Baptist Chapel, Hill Cliffe, on Sunday when the autumnal anniversary services were held. Interest was aroused by the visit of the Rev. J. Knight Chaplin, editor of the "Endeavour Times," and Secretary of the National Council of the Endeavour Movement in Great Britain, who preached at each service. His genial qualities, magnetic personality, strong sympathy with the young, and his prominent association with world-wide 'Endeavour," have gained for him the distinction of the "Young people's minister," and wherever he goer he is followed by crowds of young people. This was strikingly exem- plified on Sunday, when the various societies of Christian Endeavour in Warrington and district were well represented at Hill Cliffe. The morning service was conducted by the Pastor, Rev. J. Samuel Hughes, the children singing special hymns and Mr. Chaplin giving an address upon "The five stones from the sling of David." In the afternoon the chapel was well filled, and Mr. Chaplin gave an excellent exposition of the sixth chapter of Isaiah, dwelling in an impressive and forcible manner upon the following. points :—"Con- viction, confession, consecration, and Imitation." The feature of this service was the striking way. in which the preacher, in the enforcement of his points, broke out into suitable song—and ledthe congregation to join the same. It reminded one of Messrs. Moody and Sankey's great meetings. The choir sang the anthem "Break forth into joy" very creditably. In the evening, the chapel was crowded when service began, and Mr. Chaplin gave a discourse on "The programme of the

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly oeneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the :same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they. may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the prevalent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adapted to the needs of the mer- chant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a preparation a contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weaklychild, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give rerief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently beneficial, when other pre- parations at best only gave temporary relief. It is strongly recommended as he Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side' Giddiness, Loss of Appe- .tite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleep- lessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved yery beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness either after an illness, long confinement to ill-ventilated rooms or any other cause. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts as the system which havrt been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease.
