BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES Warrington Borough Police Court. Minor Cases.

SATURDAY.—Before Messrs. Birchall (in the chair), J W. Hayward, R. T. Fairclough and Dr. Fox. FROM WORKHOUSE TO GAOL.—Ernest William Holden, who was remanded from Thursciay,was sent to gaol for fourteen days for stealing a cotton handker- chief from the stoving room at the Workhouse.—The Chief Constable stated that this was prisoner's tenth appearance. He was one of those men who thought the world owed him a living and he meant to have it. MONDAY.—Before Messrs. Monks (presiding), Heyward Birchall, and Wright, Alderman Smethurst, and Coun- cillor Thornton. OSAD Case—George Humphries a stranger pleaded guilty to wilfully breaking a pane in a gas lamp 4in Museum-street, on Sunday night, doing damage to the amount of 2s.—Sergeant Bell said he met the prisoner, who told him he broke the glass in order to be locked up.—The Chief Constable said the prisoner was a man of superior education, and at one time was in the employ of the Bridgewater Canal Company as a clerk at Runcorn. He was suffering from slight metal aberration, possibly brought on for want of proper food and nourishment. He was living the life of a low tramp which he was not used to, and for which he was not fitted. He (the Chief Censtalele) kept him at the Police Station for protection. Upon the prisoner consenting to go into the Workhouse the charge was withdrawn. CASE WITHDRAWN.—NET. Browne, who appeared for the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children' mentioned the adjourned ease in which William Caldwell. formerly a farmer at Fearnhead, we summoned for alleged neglect. A very good improve- ment had been shown in the state of the house and the children, and as the object of the society had been attained, he wished to withdraw the summons.—The Bench agreed. EARLY PREPARATIONS FOR BONFIRE NIGHT.—Four boys named Arthur Earley, 9. Albert-street; Thomas Brown, 15 Albert-street; Frederick Douglas.ll, Albert- street, and Harry Machell, 2, Dye Houses, were summoned for wilfully damaging a hedge at Howley early in September.—James George Hatton stated that the hedge belonged to Messrs. Fairclough and Sons, by whom he was employed as a clerk. It had been sawn away, and damaged to the extent of 408.—P.C. Clough spoke to interviewing the defendants. Machell said he and Douglas had turns at sawing the hedge down. Earley and Brown admitted that they chopped the hedge down with an axe, in preparation for November sth.—The Chief Constable said he had a book full of complaints of this kind of damage.—The Chairmansaid the Bench were sorry, they could not order the boys to be birched. They would fine each ss. or in default two days' imprisonment in the , cells. They advised the parents to allow the boys to remain in custody.—Mrs. Machell : I will leave mine, sir.—The Chairman: It wi'l do him good. FIGHTING.—For fighting, James Ormond, 9, Viaduct street, Earlestown ; William Jones, 14, Nicholson street Warrington ; and Samuel and James Lee, 15. and ' 17, Bowes street, were each bound over and ordered to pay the costs. STREET FooTeara..—For playing football in Hoyle street on September 12th, James and Richard Billington 31, Catherine street, and Frederick and Peter Smith, 8, Ellis street, were fined 6s each.—P. C. Clough said the boys were playing outside Hoyle street mission, while service was going on. WEDNESDAY.—Before Mr. T. Birchall in the chair and Alderman Hutchinson. THEFT By A YOUTH.—Ftederiek Smith, 16 years of age, residing in Ellesmere-street, was charged with stealing a silver lever watch, of the value of £2, from the dwelling house of Charles Turner, 72 Ellesmere- street, between six and nine o'clock on Monday night.— Ile pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that at six o'clock on the evening in question the watch was seen in the kitchen. No doubt the kitchen was left un- occupied during the evening and the boy,who lived only a few doors away, would have ample access to it. At nine o'clock the watched was missed and a search made, and as it could not be found the police were informed. Inquiries were made by Detective Meacock, who ascer- tained that the prisoner met another boy, named Locker, in Mersey-street, and after telling him a plausible tale, got him to pawn the watch at Mr. Domville's pawnshop in Knutsford-road. The assistant in the shop lent 12s. on it, and part of the money NVIIS spent in going to the theatre. The prisoner was arrested on Tuesday, and al- though he first denied all knowledge of the theft he eventually admitted taking the watch. Nine shillings were subsequently found in the boy's boots.—The Chair- man said the Bench were sorry they could not give the prisoner the birch, and as they did not want to send him to gaol without the option of a fine he would be fined 20s. and costs.

STOCKTON HEATH'S REPRESENTATION. Decision of the Rural Council Sub-Committee. A meeting of the Sub-committee of the Runcorn Rural District Council appointed to re-consider the applicttion of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee for additional representation on the Council was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. There were present : Messrs. G. Pilling (in the chair), Joseph Knowles, Joseph Mounfleld, S. Woodward and G. F. Ashton (clerk). Mr. James Whitley and Mr. Edwin Bradshaw attended to state the case for Stockton Heath. They Pointed out that within the past five years the popula- tion and the ratable value- of the parish had very materially increased, the present population being over 3,000. Stockton Heath was the third largest district in the Union as regarded ratable value and the second as regarded population. A great many houses were at present in course of erection new streets were being formed, and the Corporation contemplated extending the tramway to the village. The County Council had already granted additional Parish Councillors, and it was contended that the cir- cumstances warranted the granting of another repre- sentative on the District Council. After a Fhort discussion, the Committee decided to recomme.;ll the District Council to make application to the Count Council for another District Councillor for Stockton ifeath. It is probable that if the Committee's decision receives the early approval of the District and County Councils,the election of another representative will take place about March next. He was more in favour of the Union children being provided for in institutions such as those he had visited since he became a member of the Board; where in addition to moral training, which was one of the first essentials, a true sense of; each individual's responsibility was taught and also what *mild enable each pupil to become a decent and in- I dependant member of society. He spoke of the excellent work done by homes which he had visited—such as Buckley Hall and Pantasaph orphanages. He alluded to the advantage of removing the children from all associations with what had been termed the taint of pauperism and said it would be wisest in the interest of public economy to train the children in that way to industrial pursuits. It was through no fault of their own that the children were in the Workhouse, and as Guardians they would fail in their duty if they did not do all they could to raise the children. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Spruce contended that they had done a great deal in that direction already. There were many work- ingmen earning from £1 to £1 ss. per week who had to keep a family on a great deal less per head than the Guardians paid for the children who were boarded out* The Chairman spoke in favour of the appointment of the Committee with the addition of Admiral Parry. So far as the cost of boarding out was concerned his feel- ing was that anything they could do for the future generation, although it might cost them a little more, would be in the interests of the ratepayers, and it would be their duty to do it. Mr. Roylance said the Roman Catholics taught the Nonconformists a lesson in the matter of providing homes. He testified to the splendid education which children received in such institutions. .

••••••• After further discussion the appointment of the Com- mittee, with the addition of Admiral Jones-Parry, was sanctioned by 10 votes to 8.

St. Alban's, Warrington.

The annual sermons on behalf of the mission were' preached on Sunday by the Very Rev. Prior Koos, O.P. of St. Sebastian's, Manchester. At the 11 o'clock Mass, before a large congregation, the rev, gentleman, in the course of an eloquent sermon, dwelt at length on the religious and moral duties of a Catholic' emphasising upon the effect Catholic example has on the number of conversions to the one true faith in this country. The Rev. Father Swarbreck, 0.9.8., celebrated High Mass (Missa Cantata). One of the chief features of the service was the masterly manner in which the choir sang Beethoven's Mass in C. Mrs. Burrows (soprano), Mrs. J. B. Caldwell (alto), Messrs. J. M. Bate (tenor), and F. Warburton (basso), comprising a capable quartette. The offertory piece, Winter's "0 Jean," was excellently rendered by Miss Eckersley (soprano), Mrs. J. B. Cald Nell (alto), and Mr. J. M. Bate (tenor). Mr. J. B. Caldwell presided at the organ with his usual skill. The evening service was also well attended, the Very Rev. Prior preaching eloquently and in a very im- pressive manner on the Divine love of the Sacred Heart of Jesus showing that by reading the Holy Gospels frequently, and through the medium of the most Holy Rosary, thereby learning how great was this Divine love we would be drawn towards the Sacred Heart of our Blessed Lord with a similar love and devotion. At the close of each sermon the preacher appealed strongly on behalf of the mission.

MOTHERS AND THEIR DAUGHTERS.

Miss Mary Bullar, of Southampton, forwarded a printed circular letter seeking the approval of the Guar- dians to a proposal for making mothers legally respon- sible for the moral conduct of their daughters up to the age of 16. She pointed out that the law recognized already that girls should be protected by punishing heavily those who offended against them, and she sug- gested the extension of this protection by making the mothers' neglect a punishable offence.—A list of 30 Boards of,Guardians who had signified approval was ap- pended. Mr. Wright said the matter must have been well con- sidered by the Boards who had approved the sugges- tion, and he therefore thought the Runcorn authority would be doing more good than harm by approving it. Mr. Woodward said the Board might approve of the principle but whether Miss Bullar's proposal was practicable or not was another question. The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi moved that the matter be referred to the Parliamentary Committee who would report to the Board. Mr. Wright proposed and Mr. Mounfield seconded that the Board approve the proposal. Mr. Woodward regarded the proposal as being rather Utopian. It seemed to him that it would not be such a very easy thing to bring criminality of that descrip- tion against a mother. He considered it to be a type of grandmotherly legislation—an interference with the rights of the home. If they penalized the mothers who were to the I disadvantage of the public criminally negligent it would be entirely different. If they were to do themselves justice and maintain the dignity of the Board they must be careful in what they supported. The proposition that the matter be referred to the Parliamentary Committee was carried.

PROPOSED EXTENSION OF THE POWERS OF GUARDIANS.

The following resolution was forwarded from the Cockermouth Guardians :—" That the Clerk be instructed to write the Local Government Board re- questing the Board to introduce legislation extending the powers of the Boards of Guardians witha viewito enable them to recover, where they have not already power to do so, and from persons whether above or below the age of 21 years, the cost of medical and other relief when rendered necessary through immorality and to detain persons in the Workhouse, at the discretion of the Board, where it is considered that there is reason for such detention." The letter was referred to the Parliamentary Com- mittee.

Tewas decided that the Chairman and Clerk should represent the Board at the forthcoming conference of Poor Law Unions.

THE BOARD'S SUBSCRIPTION TO THE NURSING ASSOCIATION.

The Clerk reported that the Board's subscription to the National Nursing Association was now due. It was usual to contribute two guineas, but when applications were recently made to tke association, they had not beau able to provide nurses, and the question was whether it was worth giving a subscription.

After some discussiiin it 'Was decided that the Clerk should write inquiring whether the association would be able to meet the requirements of the Board in the future The subscription would be withheld pending a reply.

TH E BOARDING OUT OF CHILDREN. INTERESTING DISC USSION. LETTER FROM MR. LINAIC ER.

Mr. Wooit again intpoduced the question of the boarding out of the children now in the Workhouse. He said certain members of the Board were under the im- pression that he was desirous of the appointment of a committee to consider a scheme such as was suggested by Admiral Jones-Parry—that the Board should purchase 15 acres of land and erect cottage homes. That was not so. Such a scheme would be too costly for such a small Union. One member had sarcastically .remarked that "while they were at it they ought to provide the chil- dren with lawn tennis and croquet grounds and a bicycle each." (Laughter.) He did not think they need fear that the Runcorn Guardians would be so extravagant as that. They. would all admit that their present system was not an ideal one. Its chief recommendation was that it perhaps cost a little less than any others, but on the other hand its chief indictment was that it gave preferential advantages to children of one creed. He ' did not think this was right when the money came out of one purse. They had only about 40 children in the house to cater for and it would be too expensive for the Guardians to erect cottage homes for their accommoda- tion. He proposed that a committee should be appointed to consider a scheme of boarding out all the children, irrespective of creed, on some basis of equality. From inquiries he had made at various homes he thought they would have no difficulty in doing this and the cost would be very little more than the cost of maintenance in the house. He moved that the following gentlemen con- stitute the committee ;—Messrs.Roylance,Lea,Douglas, Linaker, Mclntyre, Darlington, Lewis, Wright, Collier, Woodward, Spruce, Antwiss, Mounfield, and Wood. The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi seconded, one of his reasons being that the children when sent away to homes would I no longer be tainted with the title :of "poor paupers" land "children of the workhouse." He referred appre- ciatively to the action of the Board in sending Roman Catholic children to homes and hoped that the benefits would be extended to children of other denominations.

Mr. Spruce considered that the children'were already respectable and well-cared for and he did not think the present was a proper time to add an additional burden to the ratepayers when the Board would be going out of office in six months. He moved that the Board con- sider the matter in committee at the close of the ordinary business at the meeting a month hence. Mr. Edwards seconded.

Admiral Jones-Parry explained that his idea was that land should be bought in the neighbourhood at a reason- able cost. The homes need not be built at once and in the meantime inmates of the house could be employed on the land. The house was now full and they ultimately would have to enlarge it or provide homes for the boys and girls. The Clerk then read a letter from Mr. C. E. Linaker expressing .regret that he was unable to attend the meetinc, especially as the question of boarding out chil- dren in the Workhouse would be under discussion. This was a very wide and complex question which must be viewed from many aspects. Their object and aim should be to adopt that policy which was most Suited to place the children under their control on a higher platform, so far as the training of the mind was concerned and to tit them for becoming really useful members of society and thus in future reduce pauperism. The train- ing should be such as would not make them despise labour but give them the conviction that in labour there was dignity; and while the mind was being trained to teach them to lead trir and noble lives, if only in a humble sphere, the hand

A Septuagenarian's Death. The death is announced at the age of 74 years of Mr. Henry Heald, who for over a period of 42 years had been in the employ of Messrs. Mackie and Co., as lithographer. A few months ago deceased had a serious illness, from which he partially recovered. He was able to follow his employment until a week ago, when he was seized with influenza and bronchial affection. He passed away at his residence in Church- street on Sunday evening. Mr. Heald, was a life-long teetotoller, and an earnest worker on behalf of the temperance cause.—The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Wednesday iafternoon, the Rev. John Yongc conducting the service. Deceased was a mem- ber of Wycliffe P.S.A. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING Or TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Theft from a Farmer's Lurry. Timothy Travis and Joseph and Samokel Whitehead, three youths residing at Burton-terrace, Burtonwood, were charged at the St. Helens Police Court, on Saturday, with stealing produce from a farmer's luny passing through Parr to St. Helens market. Sergeant Jackson stated that in consequence of a complaint by Mr. Edward Marshall, of Rixton Moss, near Warring- ton, he went together with a constable on Friday even- ing to Collins Green, and followed Mr. Marshall's lurry along the road. When Parr Flat was reached the three prisoners went behind the luny and untied the tarpaulin covers and extracted a quantity of pears. They were then pounced on by the officers. The boys and their parents promised amendment, and the youths were each bound over in £1 to be of good behaviour. The parents were also bound over. BEST BOOTS FOR ALL.—UNDER the CLOCK.—Advt. ASK fOr CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLA.Ss IVINERAL WATERS.—Advt.

This is the fine old fallacy which was knocked on the head by Mr. Chamberlain and others in 1885. It assumes that England, under the influence of Cobden, Peel, and Gladstone, adopted Free Trade asa half-measure, in the hope, which experience has falsified, that the rest of the world would adopt it too. The truth is, as a very slight study of the debates in 1846 will chew, that the Corn Laws were abolished because they were injurious to the United Kingdom and not as a concession to foreigners, or as part de bargain with other States. Free Trade does not mean the natural exchange of pro- ducts on equal terms. It means a tariff for revenue only, and its ethical foundation is that Parliament has no moral right to talc the people for the benefit of a particular trade. Every Protective duty costs the con- sumer more than it brings into the Treasury, because it is only levied upon foreign goods, whereas it raises the price of home goods also. Peel and Cobden knew all about the doctrine of retaliation. It is nothing new. Peel himself tried it for the purpose of inducing foreigners to lower their tariffs. But it completely failed. A tariff war resulted in raising tariffs all round, and then the great Minister, who could learn as well as teach, who learnt from Cob- den and taught Gladstone adopted the sound principle that hostile tariffs must be fought by free imports. Why? Because retaliation injures those who employ it more than those against whom it is employed. To quote the accurate and ingenious illustration of Sir Edward Grey, it is a weapon with a blunt point and a sharp handle. Germany, the United States, France, Italy, and all British Colonies that possess self-govern- ment, impose Protective duties on British manufac- tures. Those duties are undoubtedly disadvantageous to British exporters. Without them the foreign market would be more easily reached. But not one penny of them falls upon any Englishman living at home. They are respectively paid by the German, the American, the French, the Italian, and the Colonial consumer. It is predicted, contrary to all experience, that the mere threat of retaliation would make foreigners abandon or mitigate their Protective system. If so, well and good. But nobody has better cause than has Mr. Chamberlain to know that bluff is a dangerous game. To threaten reariation, and not to act upon the threat would make England ridiculous in the eyes of the world. We are bound to assume that, in some cases at all events, the threat would becarried out. What would happen thee? Instead of one evil there would be two and the second evil would be worse than the first. France taxes our iron, or, rather the French Legislature taxes those Frenchmen who buy it. By the law of re- taliation we should tax French silk. In other words, Parliament would make Englishmen and Englishwomen pay more for silk,-not because the money was wanted for public purposes, but because France was to be punished for taxing British iron. Could their be a more ridicu- lous example of cutting off one's nose to spite one's face.

A GOOD- RESOLUTION.—Wear ALF TAYLOR'S Wonderful Boots.—Advt.

In one respect Mr. Chamberlain has been, since his speech at Birmingham last May, perfectly consistent. He will not tax raw material, Raw material provides work for the people, and must therefore be left alone. But to retaliate on any large scale without taxing raw material is impossible. As has been well said, the manufactured article of one trade is the raw material of another. Take leather. Compared with hides, it is a manufactnred article. Compared with boots it is raw material. Paper is the manufactured product of rags. It is the raw material of books. As Bastiat, the clear- est and wittiest of economists,says, the umbrella-maker wants Protection for umbrellas, but he demands Free Trade in wood, silk,fand whalebone. The manufacturer has acquiesced in Free Trade because as a sensible man, 1 he perceives that unimpeded access to the markets of the world for his raw materials is more important to him in the long run than Protection against the foreign competition with his industry. But if there is to be retaliation, every trade will clamorously require that it shall be directed against the corresponding trade abroad. Free Trade is fair to every- one. Under a protective tariff, without which there can be no retaliation, every trade that is not protected has a legitimate grievance. "Taxing the foreigner "is a specious cry. Unfortunately it is a cry, and nothing besides. If we could tax foreigners, and make them pay for our Army, our Navy, our Civil Service, there would be no reason why we should pay any taxes ourselves. There would indeed, be only one drawback to our per- fect financial felicity, and that would be the fact that by parity of reasoning the foreigner could tax us. The real aim of Protection is always artificial scarcity. If it did not raise prices by limiting supply nobody would care for it. Such is the object and such is the effect of the tariffs, against which retaliation has been threatened. If such were not the object, such at least would be the effect of retaliation itself. The merchants of London in 1820, when Cobden was a boy at school,petitioned the House of Commons that they might have leave to buy in the cheapest market and sell in the dearest. Protection was a denial of „the former privilege. Retaliation would be the same. But as the reconstituted Government really in favour of taxing the foreigner? This question can be answered by a simple and practical test. There is one way in which foreigners may tax themselves, and have taxed themselves, for our benefit. Bounties on the export of goons imported into this country confer a positive advantage upon the British consumer, because they lower prices. The most conspicuous instance of this practice was the premium given by the Anstrian,Germa.n, Russian, and other Governments for the export of sugar. Such a policy is neither more nor less protectionist, neither more nor less consistent with Free Trade, than the high tariffs at which measures of retaliation, if adopted, would bcfaissed. The difference between them is this. Protective duties, though they injure most the countries which levy them, are still inlurious in their degree to the over-sea trade of the United Kingdom. The bounties on sugar, besides cheapening it to the British consumer, were a valuable aid to the dealers in biscuits, jam, and the many other provisions of which , sugar forms an ingredient. A Free Trader cannot de- fend bounties on principle. He could not advise that his own country should give them. But inasmuch as we bear patiently with hostile tariffs, believing that the ' remedy would be worse than the disease, so we should, . if we had been wise' have thankfully accepted as a set- off the subsidy which foreign taxpayers contributed to , our own industrial system. What, however, have Mr. Balfour and his colleagues done? They have joined a foreign Conference to pro- ' mote the suppression of sugar-bounties from which we derived nothing but good. They have succeeded by threats of countervailing duties in procuring the repeal of the bounties on Austrian and German sugar. They , have exoluded Russian and Argentine sugar altogether. ; They have put it in the power of a foreign syndicate, . ; where the British representation is as one against ten, i to decide whether the laws of any State are so framed : I that its sugar is bounty-fed, and must therefore be ex- • cluded from British soil. At the same time, manufac- tured articles for which sugar is used are freely admitted to compete with similar articles made at home. That they ought to be so admitted no Free Trader will deny. But to admit them while artificially limiting the supply sf foreign sugar is essentially . unjust, and a subject of reasonable com- , .plaint to eVery British confectioner. The . bugar Convention; which now rests upon the Authority , of an Act of Parliament, is the most foolish form of , retaliation which could possibly be devised. The only , excuse made for it was that it would save our West, Indian Colonies, or rather the Capitalists who had inves- ted their money in West Indian sugar, fiom ruin. But ; this plea, even if it were inadequate is not well founded , • land will not bear examination. The great market for . , West Indian sugar is the United States, and the sugar , I excluded from Europe will go to increase the competi- ' I tion there. Of the three Commissioners sent by the pre- sent Government to study the subject on the spot two, Sir David Barbour and Sir Edward Grey, reported I against countervailing duties, to say nothing of exclu- , I , sion. This lesson in retaliation ought to be enough. But there are other practical reasons against it besides the , plain teachings of economic science. When the "Fair Traders" started their agitation in 1883 trade was bad, ' and, as hard cases made bad law, so bad trade is favour- ' able to quack remedies. Is our trade declining now? To judge it from experts alone betrays elementary , ignorance. Its volume can only be determined by addine • ! exports and imports together. An excess of imports • over exports means that we are making a good bargain; that we get more than we give; that we buy cheaply and sell clearly. But though our exports are far below ! our imports they have increased, and not diminished.

Proposed Works Draughts Competition. R. A. Naylor, E sq., president of the -Warrington Draughts League, having generously promised to provide a championship trophy for the League, in place of the "Chalmers " Cup (which was won out- right by the Warrington D. and C.C. last season), the Committee have decided to throw the competition for the new cup open to the various works in the town, and hope that advantage will be taken by the draughts players employed at these works to enter teams in the League, and thus add more interest to the competition and aid practically in promoting and popularising the game as a 'inter pastime. The annual subscription to the league is only 28. 6d. for each team; and the want of a club room suitable for playing their "home" matches in need not deter works teams from entering the competition, as in such cases an arrangement can be made for playing their matches "away." Draughts players at any of the works desirous Of •entering the competition are .invited to send a representative to a special meeting of the League; to be held at Cairo- street school room, on. Monday evening, October 11t1, at 8 pan. Any further information may be obtained from Mr. D. .Plinston (hon. sec.), 22, Priory- street, or from F. Dunne (chairman), 6, Manchester- road, Warrington. Warrington Man found Drowned.

On Monday night Mr. Coroner Dobson held an in- quest at the Aston Arms, Sutton, near Runcorn, on the body of Frank Wakefield,of 23,Naylor street,Warring- ton, who was found drowned in a millstream of the river Weaver. The widow Mary Ann Wakefield, said deceased left home at 7.45 on Tuesday morning to go to his work at Wilderspool Brewery, and she heard nothing more of him until Saturday, when she was told that he was drowned. He had no trouble that she knew of, and she could not account for his leaving home. Mr. Dykes, foreman of the jury said Ithe deceased was in his hotel on Tuesday, and said he had had some bother with his wife about the children. Police-Sergeant Bennett said Samuel Dutton, gardener, last saw the deceased alive. He gave deceased some apples, and deceased paid for a drink at the Bridge Hotel, but did not drink himself. They left the hotel together, and deceased; who was quite sober, said he was going to Preston Brook. There were no marks of Violence on the body, and in the pockets was some silver money, a watch and chain, some apple, and other articles.—William Stubits, a labourer, said he fon ad a cap and clay pipe on the stream bank. The Coroner said the affair was a complete mystery. Deceased might have slept on the bank of the stream and rolled in. An open verdict was returned.

DEADLY DAMP HOUSES. The Result of a Wat Season.

The heavy rain-falls of the present year have caused dismay among householders generally, for damp has penetrated the walls of living and sleeping rooms to a considerable extent, and the effect has been noticeable in the great increase of complaints following colds and chills. These consequences frequently prove more than temporary. An instance is reported horn Derby, on the authority of the "Derby Express," in which a man, living in a damp home, fell so ill that his life was draired of, and for three years his health was affected, until in fact, strength was restored by a course of Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. Mr. John Cotton, who now lives in comfort at 27, Kedleston-road, Derby, gave the following account of his experience of a damp horst* to the reporter : "Three years ago," he remarked, "I took a house which I found was damp, especially the bedroom. After living there only a few weeks T began to feel the ill effects; I was sick, had no appetite, and turned against food, and felt so miserable and weak that I had to give up work for two months. At the, end of the summer, being no better, I went to Scarborough, hopine, the change would benefit me. But I became worse there, and returned home. I managed to resume work for a time, but then the crisis came. I had to take to my bed and call in a doctor, who said that I had gastric ulcer and that•my heart was diseased. At this time I was quite unable to take solid food. " Then my friends sent for a physician, who told my wife that I was almost bloodless, and that my case was hopeless. Another doctor, said that hehad never known so serious a case as mi. A second specialist was con- sulted, for the palpitation of the heart was very bad. Various treatments were tried for months, with some improvement at times, but in October, 1902, my health had quite broken again.' "Then my brother persuaded me to try Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people; in fact,he bought me the first box being so convinced of their merits. After the third box was empty I could feel the pills had done me a lot of good." "In what way did you feel a change ?" "The sickness left me, my appetite returned, and food nourished me. I could walk without distress, though when I was ill going upstairs made me quite breathless. Now I can run up. I eertainly attribute my present good health to Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, for T have not felt so well as I do now for over three years." Damp, cold and windy weather lowers the vitality and induces ailments the system would otherwise overcome. Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people supply strength and enable the system to withstand the changes of our climate. In countless instances they have cured rheumatism, sciatica, general muscular weakness, all diseases arising from impoverishment of the blood, ancemia, eczema; rickets, consumption, "decline," indigestion, and palpitations. As a nerve tonic they are unequalled, and have cured St. Vitus' dance, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, early decay, and ladies' ailments. Substitutes are worthless ; refuse to accept them, and try another shop. Any respectable dealer will supply the genuine pills if you ask for Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, but in case of diffi- culty send direct to Dr. Williams' medicine company, Holborn-viaduct, London, enclosing two and nine for one box, or thirteen and nine for six boxes. With this safeguard at hand the effects of damp need not be feared.

SEQUEL TO A FIRE, The Damage to the " Royal Oak " Inn,

Bridge-street.

Before the commencement of the ordinary business at the Warrington Police Court on Monday, Mr. A. Browne made an application on behalf of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co. He stated that some few Months ago a brush works in Bridgestreet was burnt wn. The adjoining "Royal Oak" Inn, belonging to ula clients, also caught fire and four rooms were destroyed. These had not been rebuilt and the tenant was clamouring for re-instatement. There were no transfer sessions until November 2nd, but Messrs. William and Segar Owen had drawn up plans for the rebuilding of the premises. Slight alterations were suggested, but he submitted that they did not come Within the section which compelled Messrs. Greenall to apply at the transfer sessions. The section in question referred to alterations which gave increased facilities for drinking. The plans had been submitted to the Chief Constable and Mr. G. F. F. White (magis- trates' clerk) and the alterations would no doubt involve two square yards more than were included in the old Premises. If the Bench, after hearing Mr. Owen, thought that was an increase within the meaning of the section the application would have to be postponed until November 2nd, but he submitted that it was an extra- ordinary case.

The r-m—an : Are the two yards increase of area inside the house.

Mr. Browne: Yes, in the smoke room. The Chairman: How can you get over that fact? Mr. Browne, after consulting with Mr. Owen, said the increase could be done away with by rounding off tile corners of the room. The area would then be ex- actly the same as before. On this understanding the magistrates granted the application.

A FATHER'S CALLOUSNESS. Spent an Invalid Child's Money on

Drink. SMART SENTENCE.

A smart sentence was inflicted in a very bad case of I...araily neglect, which oocupied the attention of Mr. F. ~Ivintiks and other justices at the Warrington Police `-' -,..,nurt on Monday. The defendant was a labourer named William Shelmerdine, of Brick-street, and he was Charged with neglecting his two children in a manner Vltely to caude them unnecessary suffering and injury to nealth.—lle pleaded not guilty. Mr. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., and said defendant could earn £1 per week if he Chose to work. The case first came under Inspec- tor Hackett's observation in June last, and he ascertained that during the two months previous defendant had given his wife on an average 6s. Per week. The boy Frederick, five years of age, was suffering from hip disease, and required food and nourishment. The house was clean and fairly well _fiumished but defendant had pawned the bed clothes. ".I.'here was no food or nre in the house and defendant Was out drinking. Inspector Hackett saw him and he promised to give up drinking and go to work. The case was kept .under supervision and there was an improve- ment in the man's conduct for about two months. In August he began to drink again and last week he was drunk on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday. There was no food in the house. Dr. Fox stated in a letter that the boy Frederick required attention and nourishment but the defendant's wife said she could not get food because her husband would not work. Mrs. Shelmerdine was called and stated that on one occasion when defendant earned 255. he gave her only 55. 6d. The boy Frederick was taken to the Infirmary. He had a money box and people who visited him, in- cluding his father, put pennies into it. One day witness and defendant's + sister went to the Infirmary and found the defendant there drunk. He had got the boy's clothes and was about to take him from the institution. The nurses and the.doctor triedto persuade him to allow the Child to stay there but he would not. The boy. after: Wards told witness that his father took the pennies out of the money box and spent them at a beer house. Defendant was "never sober "during August. The Chairman asked how it was that aTdrunken Man Could go and take a child out of the Infirmary. Mr. Browne said that as defendant was the parent he ;lid not see how the-Infirmary officials could refuse to let nlin take the child. Mr. Birchall said he would inquire into the case. Mr. F. Aukland stated that he visited the house and found no food in it. Winifred Cooper,a neighbour,said she kept defendant's Defendant and one of the children for sixteen weeks. keep told her that "he did not marry his wife to ",.el3 her. He only married her for love." (Laughter). Inspector Hackett also gave evidence. Defendant called his mother who stated that the Children had nnt been neglected as she herself had attended to them. L 'lle chairman said this was one of the worst cases the tench had had to deal with for a very long time. "gond-int must go to gaol for six months with hard labour.'

WHY IT WON'T WORK HERE.

HOW IT WORKS IN GERMANY.

What is a Zollverein?

IT_is a strange thing that Mr. Chamberlain,

who has made a-reputation by attacking foreign countries, should have had to go to a foreign country for a political programme. The Birmingham supply having apparently run out with Old-Age Pensions he has had to go abroad to replenish his stock. He has dumped a German policy into the midst of our domestic concerns. Mr, Chamberlain apparently, despised our " parochial " interest in educa- tion. If he had made a few inquiries in Germany he would have found that it was education which enabled the German youth to battle with the wretched conditions which Protection imposes. But because Mr. Cham- berlain despised, or affected to despise, our educational struggles, he dumped this German Zollverein into our midst and said, There, give up talking about British education and talk about a German Zollverein.

Hasty Conclusions. MR. CHAMBERLAIN has shown no evidence of having thought out any part of his proposals. He has made so many miscalculations that it is only charitable to conclude he has not made any real calculations at all. The probability is that the whole business is a monster red- herring to keep people from inquiring into the working of the Education Act, the state of South Africa, and the condition of the War Office. But for once the red-herring, big and strong though it may be, has failed to accom- plish its purpose. To all his miscalculations Mr. Chamberlain has made the mistake of thinking the electors can .only think of one thing at a time. Small matters may pass out of their minds in the face of some big question. But the Education Act, the War Office, and the Zollverein are all big things, none of which can be forgotten, and any one of which would be strong enough to destroy. the biggest majority that ever existed.

As in Germany. Jr is, therefore, quite a minor miscalcula- tion we have to point out, that what may suit Germany would be a very bad thing for Great Britain. The Zollverem, which Mr. Chamberlain asks us to adopt, is admirably suited for a number of states which border one upon another, as England borders upon Scotland and Wales. Everybody admits that a Zollverein is necessary in such cases. It would he a mad idea if the three countries which make up the United Kingdom were to set up duties against each other's goods. But such were the conditions which existed in Germany prior to 1833. The Zollverein swept away all the barriers between the German States, and established Free Trade—perhaps Mr. Balfour might call it insular Free Trade —between all the various countries which make up thq German Empire. Such as it was, it was a tl:4i,tit victory for Free Trade. The Germal, P.t•l'.), instead of taxing each other's goods. ,1-• --,ined to establish Free Trade among .11,imoivcs.

Th,e Colonies Repudiate. Now, if Mr. Chamberlain had taken any trouble really to look into the question, he would have at once seen that while a Zollverein is all right in Germany it is im- possible within the British Empire. In the first place, the Colonies are not prepared to establish Free Trade within the Empire, in the same way that the German States have done. They are as anxious to keep British goods out of the Colonies as they are to exclude German goods. It does not matter to them, they say, whether they are under- sold by British or German manufacturers, the effect is all the same. The Colonies have absolutely refused even. to consider a Zollverein, or Free Trade within the Empire; and so there the proposal ends, so far as they are concerned. But Mr. Chamberlain is not content, or at least he says he is not, to allow the matter to end there. He says we must allow. the Colonies to send in their goods free,whjle they are to tax ours so heavily that British manufactures will be almost completely shut out of Colonial markets. It is a sort of "heads I win, tails you lose" bargain.

Life I'l Germany. BUT if Mr. Chamberlain ignores the obvious lessons which Protection teaches in Ger- many, there is no need why we should do so. A brief examination of the conditions of the lives of the workers in Germany will speedily convince the workers of this country that they have nothing to gain and everything to lose by adopting the German policy. First let us compare the wages of a farm servant in this country with those of the Fatherland: . The British labourer earns about 15s. a week. The German labourer earns about 10s. a week.

But wages is only half the problem. It is not so much what a man earns; it is what he can buy. Take the price which the German has to pay for his corn. Last June corn was 355. 6d. a quarter in Germany, while it was 275. 6d. in this country the fact being that under Protection there is a tax of is. nd. per quarter upon all corn going into Germany, so the labourer has to pay so much more for his food. Indeed, wages are so low, and food so dear, that in order to make a living not only the labourer, but his wife and children, have to go into the fields. Between them they may earn £lO more in the course of a year.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
The Worker's Food.

WHEN' bread is dear there is little money to buy meat. Our own workers have little enough of the good things of life, but their lot is bright indeed when compared with that of the German under Protection. In this country the amount of money spent upon meat per head is £2 12s. In Germany it is not half that amount; it is only £1 4s. That is, a German family of five persons can only spend 4d. a day on meat—less than a penny per day for each person. But that is not the worst of it. While the workers in Great Britain get good honest beef, mutton, and bacon, the Germans have to put up with horse-flesh, and even in the recent bad times with dog-flesh! This is what Protection has brought upon Germany—low wages and bad food. There are some living who can remember the time when things were very little better in this country. Under Protection our people had to Jive upon rye-bread. If we 'allow Mr. Chamberlain to have his way we shall have to ,go back to rye-bread once more.

The Isle of Wight County Council Education Com- mittee have decided, with the consent of the Bishop of Winchester, to arrange religious teaching on one day each week by the Church authorities, the Free Church people to enjoy the same facilities.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday September 26, 1903.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
'THE FREE CHURCHES

THE FORTHCOMING ELECTIONS.

Everywhere in Free Church circles prepara- tions are being made for the forthcoming elec- tions--Parliamentary and municipal. With Cabinet resignations and general unsettlement, it seems to be more and more certain that the General Election cannot be long delayed, and carefully laid plans must be in existence as soon as possible, so that when the date is announced all may be ready for a strenuous battle. The National Free "Church Council are issuing in- structions to the local Councils all over the country in view of the coming fight. They point out very clearly that, whilst in the ordinary way the National Council could not take part, as an organised force, in a General Election, the occa- sion is altogether extraordinary. The Govern- ment has simply ignored the Free Churches. Despite the most emphatically expressed pro. tests of half the religious population of the country, they have, at the dictation of the clerical party, passed two educational measures inflicting grave injustice on Free Churchmen, and it is because they feel that the position is an intolerable one that the National Council feels that it is its duty not to shirk the fight. It is pointed out that the struggle is a religious one; and only on that score does it concern the Federation.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
ORGANISING THE FIGHT.

Within a short time meetings of local Free Church Councils Ili° to be held all over the Country, and q.t conferences in connection with these the proposals of the National Council will be considered, and the necessary action taken. Committees will be formed for the election, appeals made to the electors, a house- to-house canvass organised, meetings held in central districts and in outlying places, and literature distributed.

MAKING CLEAR THE ISSUES.

It is intended that every voter in the country shall be made acquainted with the position of the Free Churches in this matter, and the ssues at stake. For this purpose the National Free Church Council has prepared a vast quantity of literature. Leaflets, cartoons, posters, &c., are in preparation, and will be issued for distribution gratis.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ELECTION FUND.

Of course all this cannot be done without money, and an appeal will shortly be made for an Election Fund for supplying the expenses of Free Church cand dates where necessary, preparing and distributing literature, &c. £50,000 is the sum required, and towards this £5OOO is already promised. On all hands it is felt that serious issues involved demand the iberality of all interested in the struggle. Whilst such gigantic efforts are being put forth to flood the country with the literature of the fiscal question, it would be little short of a calamity if lack of funds should prevent the prosecution of the campaign for a national system of education.

THE MUNICIPAL CONTESTS

Great preparations are thus being made in view of the General Election, but the local contests are not being overlooked. The County, City, Urban District, and other Councils which are charged with the administration of the Education Act hold in their hands great powers which may be used on the one hand for intoler- able injustice to Free Churchmen and citizens generally, or, on the other, for as broad and fair administration as the Act is capable of. If Councils everywhere adopted the position of the Welsh and Cambridgeshire bodies, the Act would be rendered innocubus. The represen- tation of Nonconformity on some of these local bodies is shamefully inadequate; the time is now at hand when something may be done to remedy this.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
SPIRITUAL WORK NOT SUFFERING.

Whilst these great efforts are being put forth, it must not be supposed that the spiritual side of the work of the Free Church Councils is being neglected. Never in the history of the movement have the missions of the National Council been more successful. Gipsy Smith, Mr. W. R. Lane, and the Rev. J. Tolefree Parr are all booked up for missions for some time ahead, and have already started with their autumn engagements.

TILE CRY FROM MACEDONIA.

The news of the Macedonian atrocities has stirred the hearts of Free Churchmen, and sent a thrill of horror through our churches. On all hands it is felt that we should give express sion to our feelings in an organised way, and bring pressure on our own Government, besides those of the other Powers to put a stop to the extermination of a Christian people. A Com- mittee of the National Free Church Council at a meeting. almost immediately to be held, are considering a very strong resolution on the subject; other action will probably follow.

MR. MEYER TO TOUR THE COUNTRY.

Probably there is not a busier than in the whole kingdom than the Rev. F. B. Meyer, the president-elect of the National Free Church Council. Mr. Meyer's interest in the Free Church move:nent has long been well known. He is now arranging to give all his time from next January, apart from his' pastoral duties, to the work of visiting local Free Church Councils and Federations. He will commence by visiting the Federations. in this way getting into touch with practically the whole of England and Wales, as at each meeting representatives from a whole county will be pres.nt. This, he expects, will take from January to July, after which he will spend the rest of the year in visiting the local Councils.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERESTING PUBLICATIONS.

Mr. Meyer is also busily engaged in his literary work, and is publishing very shortly, through the Publication Department of the National Free Church Council, two most impor- tant volumes; one of these is especially timely in view of the education struggle. It is entitled. "The Religious Basis of the Free Church Posi- tion." It is printed in two colours and hand- somely bound (Is. net). The other one is en- titled "The Art of Life," and is publishei at 26. net.

GIPSY SMITH' SOLOS.

All who have heard the striking, tender and pathetic appeals which Gipsy Smith has made, nct only in speech. I It: in song, will be interested in another publicati )n of the National Free Church Council—" Gipsy Smith's Favourite Solos." This contains the accompaniments, and is edited and arranged by Mr. W. H. Jude (is. net).

THE ROMANCE OF METHODISM.

This month's Free Churchman contains an article by W. B. Fitzgerald on "The Romance of Early Methodism." There are few subjects of more absorbing interest than this. Metho- dism, as Mr. Fitzgerald says, being one of the few movements that give colour and glow to the Eighteenth Century. Some fine portraits of early Methodists illustrate the article.

GREAT SANKEY SCHOOL BOARD. -- -- Final Meeting of the Authority.

The Grelt Sankey School Board has now ceased to exist, the Education Act having come into operation in the district. 'The final meeting of the authority was held on Tuesday evening, the Rev. J. R. 'Jones presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. J. Fulton Smith, G. Thompson and Perks,and also the clerk (Mr. Jeans, jun.) and the Headmaster (Mr. Clarke).

===== 0002601_19030926_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
ATTENDANCES.

The Headmaster's monthly report showed that the fees amounted to £2 lis. Bd., and the average attendance for the month was 256'0.

FINANCES.

The Clerk stated that the treasurer's account book showed a balance in favour of the Board of MO9 2s. sd. That would clear the Board with the exception of the account for the building of the wall, and they would have to hand over that liability to the County Council. The County Council would receive from them a balance of about £9O, and the wall building would cost about .C 75 so that they were still on the right side. It was decided to allow the master to order some registers which were needed for the school, the account for Which, it was understood, would go to the new authority.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
INSPECTOR'S VISIT.

The Headmaster reported tWa-t'ii.M.'s head inspec- tor and the sub-inspector visited the school on Monday. They were very pleased with the school.

WORK FOR THE _NEW AUTHORITY.

Mr. Fulton 'Smith, who is'the --representative of the district on the new authority, said he had received a letter from Mr. Joseph Smith resigning his position as school attendance officer. At a meeting of a sub-com- mittee of the authority it was mentioned that possibly they could got the present school attendance officers to continue in office for a certain period. Con- tinuing, he said that at the last meeting of the anthorityhe banded in the names of the members of the Board, and really they would continue as they ware only they would be the new authority. Instructions would follow as to their duties. The Chairman: There are two more members to come from somewhere. Mr. Smith: Ihavenot asked about that.,

GFVERAL THANKS. Mr. Thompson proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman for his services since the retirement of Mr. Wright. Mr. Fulton Smith seconded. They,he said, had every reason to think that the work of the new authority would go on as satisfactorily as the old one. The new authority was certainly going to be a great expense all over the country, but if the people would look upon it as a kind of investment for their children and their children's children, it would not seem so formidable. He thought there would be better co-ordination and the change would be to theadvantage of the children. The proposition was then carried. The Chairman returned thanks, and said it had been a pleasure to go to the meetings with such willing and ready colleagueleto help him in the work. He wished to say a word of praise and commendation for their staff. The headmaster had been labouring under some diffi- culty on account of physical weakness, yet every time he (Mr. Jones) had come to the school he had found him working hard. He had stuck bravely to his work. Mr. Thompson said he would Like to include their Clerk (Mr. Jeans) in the thanks. They would miss him as he was always very courteous, and it had been a pleasure to work with him.

Mr. Jeans returned thanks. The Headmaster also referred to the assistance he had received from Mr. Jeans.

PRESENTATION TO WIDNES.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

Authors of letters intended for publication must inall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side buty of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. . Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly A short letter has a much greater chance,of insertion than a long one.

We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return themanuscripts which are not used.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
Selection of Mayor.

Sir,—As one of the Council who attended the Caucus Meeting to consider the selection of Mayor of Warrinp ton for the next year, I vobed with the majority in favour of Mr. J. C. Parr being asked to stand for the third year; and others na doubt voted the same way. hoping that he might accept the position, so as to relieve the Council of a difficulty—that is, we have no one who will accept the office. Nowsir, I think this is far from the truth ; and my short experience in the Council (and I have watched matters very closely) satisfies me a onto that we have gentlemen in the iCouncil,if they could get a unanimous vote,whojare well qualified to fill with dignity this most important office of Mayor. I will just mention three: AldermanlMonks, Alderman Bolton and,Councillor William Pierpoint, all of whom have done yeoman service to the town, and have fairly won the honour. When Mr. Parr was first elected, there was a cry amongst some members of the Council that we ought to elect the Mayor from amongst ourselves. I agree with that, and there is now a chance to bring this about. If Mr. Parr shouldreaseto accept office—and I may say at once that I am not in favour of any gentleman accepting the office for'more than 2 years, I think no one should be chosen until after the first of November next, so that the newly-elected members can have a vote in the election of the gentleman to be appointed.—Yours Scc.,

COUNCILLOR.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

Methodist Free Church, Buckley Street. As this church is undergoing renovation the harvest thanksgiving services took place in the schoolroom, on Sunday. There was a hearty response to the .appeal made by the committee for decorative materials, and -with a large stock of fruit, vegetables, corn, &c., the workers made the school look very pretty. Mr. B. Richardson, of Liverpool, preached morning and even- ing. In the afternoon a musical service was held, Mr. Jaites Shawipres i di ag,and Mr.B.Richardson gave a c,apital address. Mr. W. Clay rendered the baritone solos, The Golden pathway" and "Sun of my soul," with pleasing effect, and Miss Lily Fairhnrst gave a credit- able interpretation of "Queen Of Angels." Miss M. Plinston, A.L.C.1V1., was the accompanist. Special anthems were rendered by the choir, under the 'leader- ship of Mr. J. Ryder. There were large congregations during the day, and at the evening service many people could not find accommodation. On Monday evening a 'fruit banquet was held,Mr. E. St. Ann's Church.

The harvest thanksgiving services were commenced •at this church on Thursday evening, when a good con- gregation assembled. The church had been very taste- fully decorated, the font, lectern and pulpit receiving special attention. The following ladies were respon- -sible for the decorations :—Misses Davies, Rigbey. Bunch, Kenyon, Barton and Horroeks, Mrs. Thomas, and Mrs. Savage. The snecial preacher was the Rev. G. Peters. Vicar of St. Thomas's Liverpool, who based an interesting discourse on the words "Seed time and harvest." In speaking of the great amount of faith which farmers must have when sowing the seed and indeed in all seasons, he pointed out how this had beep exemplified recently. Up to ten days ago the farmers did not know whether they would be able to get their crops in or whether they would have to lose them. all. God in His mercy had, however, vouchsafed a period of fine weather so that most of the crops. were now safely gathered in. The preacher drew several useful lessons from the harvest and impressed upon his hearers the necessity for faith which, however, was nothing unless combined with work.—The choir rendered the anthem "Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem," Mr. E. A. Padmoth presiding at the organ.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Barnabas' Church.

This church had been beautifully decorated for the harveht thanksgiving services which commenced on 'Thursday evening. Gifts of flowers, fruit, cereals and vegetables were forthcoming in abundance and the decorations were carried out by the following ladies : Mesdames Hollingworth, Iddon, Bracegirdle, Jones, Bellian, Billineton, Carmen. Furnival, Dowling, Helsby, and Webb: Misses Whitehouse, E.A.ans, Fry. Helsby, Wilson and Jefferson. The artistic embellishment of the font called forth general admiration, A choice collection of fruit overhung a back-ground of oats, and gladioli, chrysanthemums, dalias and a profusion of other flowers encircled the top cif the font. At the base fruit and bread were arranged and there was a lovely bunch of flowers of varied tints. The pulpit had also received special attention. It was tastefully ornamented with flowers,fruit, and fringes of corn, and in the centre was a cross of white flowers. The altar was choicely deckod with flowers,and the general effect was heightened by the neat adornment of the altar rails and choir stalls. The base of the large west window ta-as covered with flowers, vegetables, and bread, and he smaller windows were somewhat similarly decora- ted. There was a large congregation at Thursday even- ing's service, the special preacher being the Rev. W. J Gardner, of Tyldesley. The rev, gentleman based his sermon .on the words "While the earth remaineth, seed time and harvest, and cold and heat, and summer and winter, and day and night shall not cease." Genesis viii, 22. He said they knew from their own experience how God's promise which formed the text had been fulfilled year by year. Some people might say "What about the harvest this year?" When they were singing that hymn "All is safely gathered in" did not some think of the heavy -rains we had had lately, of the complaints about the failures of the crops, of corn lying rotting in the fields? There might be something in that, but were they merely thanking God for what went on within a short distance of their own homes or within the boundaries of their own country? It was quite true that this year, as in other years there had been partial failures, but there had not been failures of harvests throughout the world, and we in England did not depend for supplies merely on our owu country, but we regarded the world as our field and all that God grew there was for the use of those who dwelt in the world. The preacher proceeded to impress -upon his hearers the duty of gratitude to God, and said that the harvest each year was evidence of God's con- tinual care for them.—The musical portion of the service was of a festal character, the choir rendering the anthem "Thou art God alone" (Simper). The solos were well sung by Mr. W. Woodcock (bass) and Mr. T. Evans, jun. (tenor). Mr. T. Evans presided at the organ. The service was conducted by the Vicar (Rev. 1. H. G. Hollingworth). assisted by the Rev. A. Theis. 'The collection was on behalf of the choir fund.

Y.M.C.A.

A meeting of this committee was held on Thursday tight at 31, Legh-street (Mr. Cowdell's) to consider the question of taking certain rooms which were con- sidered very central and suitable. Mr. Holland pre- sided and in the absence of the esteemed secretary (Mr. Pierce Williams), who is away on-his holidays, stated why the meeting had 'been convened. Several letters of apology for non-attendance were read. Mr. W. H. Sherburn of Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath, wrote:: —I am sorry it will be impossible for me to be with you to-night. I hope ynn will have a good time and I wish the movement every success. We all know how some such centre is needed and I pray you May be guided in the right direc- tion." The Chairman remarked how well the committee had kept together sincb the formation of the present Association, especially under the trying circumstances and considering the difficulty in obtaining rooms. However, he hoped that difficulty had now been overcome, and they looked forward to a very bright opening for the winter months. The meeting was well attended, and considerable enthusiasm was thrown into -the discussion by the younger members of the Committee. The proceedings were brought to a close with the Benediction.

DEATH OF MR. THOMAS FLETCHER.

BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH.

A Long and Useful Career.

Death has removed a distinguithed townsman in the person of Mr. Thomas Fletcher, t,C.S., founder of the firin of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell, and Conipany, gas engineers and artistic ironfounders, 9f Warrington, Pendleton, Manchester and London. Ur. Fletcher had been in declining health for some years and during the past 'twelve months had been prevented from taking an active part in the concern in which he took a whole-hearted interest. Latterly Ire had been confined to his house at Grappenhall, and towards the end of last week there were indications that he was approaching the end. On Sunday he became uncon- scious, and passed peacefully away the same evening. He was in his 64th year. His wife pre-deceased him in May, 1900. She was a daughter of John and Dinah Clare, of Worsley. Two sons and two daughters' survive. Mr. • William Fletcher, is prominently con- nected with the business, and his brother, Mr. Ernest Fletcher, is at present in Australia. The late Mr. Fletcher was born in 1840 at " Kirk- i hams," an old house four Miles from Manchester He was the son of the late Mr. John Fletcher and the youngest of a large family, the male members of which were successful in the legal and medical professions. The two eldest sons, John Shepherd Fletcher, M.D., L.S.A., and James Ogden Fletcher, M.D., F.R.C-S., M.R.C.S., L.S.A., and the two youngest sons, Joseph Lyon Fletcher. M.R.C.S., L.S.A„ and Thomas Fletcher, F.C.S., were all educated at the Manchester Grammar School. Mr. Thomas Fletcher received his matlametical . training from the late James Orrarod, who' died last year only, after having watched with extreme interest the brilliant career of his most promising pupil. Intended for the -medical profession, and having studied so far as the dissecting room, he gave it up as distasteful to him, and turned his attention to the study o; dentistry, as giving more scope for his scientific and inventive tastes.

First as pupil, then as partner with Henry Planck, formerly Dean of the dental schoo.l connected with the Victoria University. his taste for invention and chemistry applied to the rarer metals was encouraged. He invented anything he saw was needful for the use of dentists, such as fillings for teeth, blowpipes and furnaces for melting metals, etc., introducing all kinds of specialities in which gas was needed for the fuel power for labour-saving appliances) In 1872 he began to extend these experiments to appliances for the chemical and physical laboratories, and findinghis efforts successful and fully appreciated, he retired from dental practice. The first point which brought his name into promin- ence was an exceedingly simple modification of the common mouth blowpipe by which a strong platinum wire "could be fused with ease in a candle or an ordinary small lamp flame. This was stiortiy followed by the inventior of a small gas-furnace . adaptable for many purposes, the walls of which being only one inch thick retained the heat so perfectly that a crucible full of cast iron could be melted and poured in five minutes starting all cold, the furnace being held in the hand or resting on a wooden table without damage -or incon- venience. This has now been also adapted for the use of ordinary petroleum oil with equally good results and they have been made in hundreds of thousands in all sizes, to deal with to 3 cwts of cast iron at one charge. The various uses to which they are, and can be, easily adapted appear to be endless. Later his invention of the oxygen blowpipe, caused great consternation among the safe makers, and it will be remembered how he alarmed the country at one of his lectures when he informed and demon- strated to his audience that he could make a similar blowpipe to open any safe, which would work silently, but at the same time assuring them that he would not do so as it would tend to assist felonious intentions.

To Mr. Fletcher is due the discovery of the laws ruling the forms and proportions of burners consum- ing gas and air mixtures by which any burner of any power can be at once correctly designed. It was from him originally that the varieties and improve- ments in these at the present day first grew-, for the scientific and domestic application of gas as a fuel. He has been a voluminous writer of papers for Scientific Journals. His best known works, as an author are two. One is entitled "Dental Metallurgy" now in its 4th English edition. It haslalso been trans- lated into French, German and Italian, and is a recognised text book. The other notable work is "Coal Gas as a Fuel" which has reached its sth edition and is also used as a standard text book. As regards the popularization of coal gas as a fuel, Sir George Livesey, in referring to this when he was pro- posed as an honorary member of the Institute of Gas Engineers said that Mr. Thomas Fletcher had done more in this direction than any ten others, in fact he might say than all the others put together.

His bu-siness career was brilliant and successful. He commenced in the year 1872 with a staff of three workmen and he began with the manufacture of dental and labour-savinf, appliances. In the year 1881 ha built the works at Thynne-street, Warrington. This with continuous additions and extensions finally became tbo small to cope with the growing business. The amalgamation of the old firm of Thomas Fletcher and Co.' with AV. and A. C. Russell and Co. took place in 1892 when Mr. Fletcher became the Chairman of the Company which now employs a staff of over 'l,OOO lands in its various branches. In 1898 the extensive and excellent works at Wilderspool were built. The gas stove, is, of course, one 'of tha Principal mdnufactures Of the firm. Its, utility is well-known in many households, and, while it may cost a good deal if not prudently used, with care it should lead to economy. ,Mr. Fletcher used to tell of a house in which the stove sent up the gas bill from half-a-crown to thirty shillings a week. Investiga- tion was made by the foreman of the firm, who called at the house about three in the afternoon when no cooking was being done, and went straight into the kitchen. There he found every burner on the cook- ing stove alight, but when he asked the cook why she was wasting gas in such a way, the reply was that she did not waste any, as she turned all the lights out be- fore she went to bed! Another story was told of a well-known chef, who kept all the ovens with the) burners full on, even when nothing was being done, as he did not like his ovens to grow cold. This artis- tic person was wasting the hotel's gas to the extent of several hundreds a year. Mr. Thomas Fletcher (together with Sir George Livesey) was made an honorary member of the Insti- tute of Gas Engineers. He belonged to many 'learned and scientific societies and was always ever active in bringing out inventions and propositions for the edification of the scientific world. • It can justly be stated that he was a born genius and considered to be the greatest authority in the world upon the uses of gas for fuel purposes. His opinion and advice upon this particular science was sought for from all parts of the globe and he freely and readily responded. Mr. Fletcher was of a most -charitable and generous disposition and the charity he bestowed was wide- spread and unostentatious as an. instance of his liberality the gift' df £l,oooto his workpeople may be m ntioned. It may be, truly said that " His life was gentle rind the elements so mixed up in him, that nature might stand up and say to all the world, this was a man." The following personal awards were obtained by•him : Gold Medals : Society of Arts Brussells Society of Arts (Lady Siemens) international Health'kxhibition Birkenhead Exhibition International Inventions international Health Exhibition Building Trades Exhibition (Society of Architects) Adelaide Jubilee Exhibition Melbourne Paris International Food and Cookery 'Exhibi• tion 3 Dunedin 1, Silver Medals :- 1892

The Examiner, Saturday September 26 1903

Mr. Fletcher was a well-known lecturer to the Society of Arts and the following are some of the lectures he gave. "Coal Gas as a Labour-saving Agent in the Mechani- cal Trade." Society of Arts Lecture 1884. "Gas for light and heat inthe workshop." Association of Foremen Engineers. Lecture at Manchester 1885. "The use of Gaseous Fuel for Laboratory Furnaces." Society of Arts Lecture 1880. "Flames." Oxford Lecture 1890.

"A new commercial application of Oxygen." Society of Chemical Industry Lecture 1888. "Flame Contact." A new departure in water heating. Gas Institute Lecture 1888.

"Flame and Smoke." Manchester Noxious Vapours Assdciation Lecture 1888. THE FUNERAL.

The grave closed over the remains of the late Mr. Fletcher on Tuesday afternoon, when all that remained mortal was quietly interred in the family grave in Grappenhall Churchyard. The villagers showed their respect by drawing the blinds of their houses, and the representatives and workmen of the firm paid a last tribute to their departed chief by attending the funeral in large numbers. The cort4ge left the house at two o'clock. The chief mourners were Mr. William Fletcher (son), Dr. Healey, Micklehurst, Mosaley (son-in-law), Mr. Charles Clare, London (nephew), Dr. Leadbetter (deceased's medical attendant), and Mr. W. J. Forshaw (family solicitor). The Warrington works were repre- sented by Mr. John Neil and Mr. W. R. Neil, directors; Mr. W. ii. Rudge, secretary; Mr. J. A. Jackson, head clerk; Mr. Foster Jackson, cashier; Mr. J. T. Knowles, 1 representing the Midland district; Mr. Arthur Landon, chief order clerk; Mr. A. White, foreman of the mould- ing department; Mr. C. Stewart, foreman pattern maker; Mr.V. Broadhurst, foreman joiner and Mr. McDermid, stock keeper. The representatives of the Pendleton and Manchester works present were Mr. Alexander Neil, vice-chairman of the company and managing director; Mr. William Russell and Mr. A. C. Russell, managing directors ; Mr. William Russell, jun., director; Mr. G. Hurst, manager of the Deansgate show rooms; Mr. Mungall, foreman of the moulding department • Mr. Bennett, foreman of the bronzing- and. enamelling department; and Mr. McPherson, foreman of the warehouse and despatch department. 1 The works both at Warrington and Pendleton were closed at noon for the remainder of the day, and many of the workmen and their wives attended the . . funeral. Among others .... present

were Mr. Lister-Kaye, Mr. William Fiirhurat (Whitley), Mr. Farnworth (Manchester), Mr. C. Young (formerly manager of the Warrington works), Mr. R G. Henshall, Mr. Segar Owen Mr. W. H. Sherburn,Mr. James Harding, Mr. Gray, Mr. Whittaker, and Sergeant Bodle, a Crimean veteran of the Royal Artilltry who was the first man employed by the late Mr. Fletcher in 1872.

The coffin was of oak, fumed and panelled,, with brass furniture. The following was inscribed on a brass plate: "Thomas William Fletcher, born June 9th,11840. died September 20th, 1903." Messrs. Symons, (coach- man), Ormerod, (head gardener), Fleet, (late head gardener), I Fairhurst, (wheelwright), Bowers, (assistant works' manager,) Peters, (foreman of the warehouse and despatch department), acted as bearers. Mr. Bowers, and Mr. Peters, are two of the oldest employees at present with the firm. The first portion of the funeral service was conducted in the church. The officiating clergymen were the Rev. Richard Greenall,and the Rev. E. Farrar. At the grave side, the Rector performed the last rites. Many floral tributes were sent. Messrs. R. Garnett and Sons were the undertakers. AN APPRECIATION.

Mr. Thomas Fletcher, of 'Warrington, say's this week's a Christian World," was a man to whom housewives owe a lasting debt of gratitude, for he was a pioneer in the application of gas to cooking stoves. Mr. Fletcher was originally a dentist, but his taste for science and for practical experiments led him to spend much time in his little laboratory. He bib upon the device of applying the principle of the Bunsen burner to cooking stoves. This proved the beginning of a new era in gas cooking. Mr. Fletcher was a daring experimentalist. Once, many years ago, when he lectured before the Literary and Philosophical Society at Warring ton,he he Id his hearers spellbound by a demonstration of the hollow- ness of flame. Taking a handful of gun-powder he plunged his hand into a large flame, and opened his fingers so that the gunpowder was clearly visible through the flame. The expected explosion did not come. Then he put a tuft of loose guncotton on the powder in his hand and set the cotton alight. The guncotton flashed up, but the gunpowder remained unexploded. Likewise he hung festoons of guncotton all over his face, body and arms, and had the cotton lighted to prove that the combustion of uncompressed guncotton is too speedy to singe human flesh. Mr. Fletcher was a big, quiet man, very deaf and extremely retiring.

They had confidence in their head teachers, and if an occasion did arise let the exception be dealt with, but he did not see his way to vote for the resolution, because it appeared to hurt the feelings and the self- respect of the head teachers. He would like to suggest that the matter be referred back to the Finance Com- mittee or rejected until another case arose which would make the arrangement inapemtive. Colonel BUCKTON supported the resolution and pointed out that the new arrangement would not necessitate much extra work on the part of the head teachers as they had already to supply a weekly return of school pence and so on. He did not think the resolution cast a slttr on the head teachers. In all public services there was a smiliar rule. On the other hand the arrange- ment would hen great help to many head teachers. They would have no discretion in the matter of recording absentees, and could not, therefore, be considered hard or severe on the other teachers. •

Mr. Jor,LEy, in reply to Councillor James Evans, said he was very loth to believe evil of anybody and when his attention was called to the speeches of Coun- cillors Loreand Horrocks at the last Council meeting with regard to the account he felt hurt that menholding public positions should indulge in what he held to be reckless mis-statements of that kind. He addressed a courteously-worded letter to Colmcillor Horrocks inviting him to give the information, to be laid before the Finance Committee, on which the charges were based. He waited a week and received no reply. He addressed a further letter to Councillor Horrocks asking for the information in order that he might lay it before the next meeting of the Committee. To that he had received no reply. What to make of it he did not know. He did not want to use any inflam- matory language, but it was difficult to criticise mis- statements of that kind without using language that was unparliamentary. He would say no more about it, but leave these people to their own conscierwes, and leave every honest citizen to think what he was disposed to think alTut their behaviour.
