• THE SECONDARY SCHOOL. Results of the Examination for • Scholarships. INSPECTORS' REPORT.

The results of the examination held at the Technical School on September 11th and 12th for Secondary School scholarships are now to hand, together with the report of the inspectors. The following is a copy of the . report:— To the Chairman and members of the School Manage- ment Committee. Gentlemen,—We have the honour of presenting our report of the examination held at the ' Technical School on the 11th and 12th September, 1903. For the Council scholarships (Class A) 61 candidates presented themselves and for the admission (Class B) 84 candidates. In Class A4l may be regarded as having reached a standard qualifying for a scholarship, and the number of places available may be awarded to the candidates in order of merit. Those failing to reach ' scholarship standard 'should also be rejected for studentship,unless they are young,and earn 40 per cent of full - marks. This would apply to nine candi- dates. in Class B, 13 are rejected on account of attainments. The majority of the - remain- ing candidates are quite promising pupils, and ' are relatively better qualified than Class A. We regard the results of the examination as showing that the general condition of Elementary Education in Warrington reaches a satisfactory level, and that good . materials for Secondary Education can be drawn from the Elementary Schools. The subject that shows to least advantage is needlework, the best exercises show- • lag the sewing neat, but the work incomplete. The greater number however,had the wrong side of the patch badly done, the patch put on the wrong way of the material, and the wrong method of a print patch entirely. Drawing is generally marked by freedom and accuracy of outline and good proportion. Many of the pupils are intelligent in grasping the point of a paragraph, and possess considerable powers of expression, both in the composition exercise, and in the answers to the geo- graphy and history questions. In arithmetic about 25 per cent of Class A. get good marks and nearly 75 per cent in Class B. This seems to show that progress in this subject is not well maintained after a certain point Grouping of higher classes is the probable cause in some cases. We were pleased to note the earnest appli- cation of the candidates under examination, and we I wish to record ourappreciation of the excellent aminge- I meats made by Mr. Murray.—We have the honour to I be, Gentlemen, your obedient servants, A. WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

MR. A. J. JOLLEY AND "RECKLESS MIS-STATEMENTS."

The Attendance of Teachers.

Mr. F. Monks presided at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee, which was held at the Town Hall on Monday evening. There were, also present :—The Revs. John Yonge, E. J. Willcocks and Morley Stevenson, Aldermen Bennett, Monk, and Smethurst, Councillors Ellison, Davies, J. Ro,bert, Brough, Wilkinson, James Evans, Brophy, Upson and Dodds, Colonel Buckton, Messrs. T. May, E. Plinston, R. T. Fairclough, J. Fulton Smith, A. J. Jolley, J. Bibby, G. Walker, J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer) and J. Moore Murray (director of education.)

THE APPORTIONMENT OF FEES. LETTER FROM CHURCH OF ENGLAND FOUNDATION MANAGERS.

The DIRECTOR read the following letter:—"At a meeting of the Association of Foundation Managers of Church of England schools for the Borough of Warrington held on Friday last, I was instructed to convey to you, in order that you might bring the matter before the next committee meeting, the resolution arrived at by the meeting respecting the proportion of fees to be refunded to the managers of the schools. The following is the resolution: 'lt was resolved that the secretary write to the Director of Education and Secretary of the Education Committee to say that a more equitable scheme would be that a fourth of the fees should be refunded to schools where the school fee reaches 3d. or upwards a week; and a half when the fee per week is less than 3d.' I trust that your committee may favourably consider the resolution above, and thus ensure that the sums refunded to the different schools may be mbre closely approximate in amount than would be the case if one-fourth of the htuount of fees were returned irrespective of the amount of fees charged.— Yours, &C., JAMES DAV/ES." . The CHAIRMAN thought this was a matter they might fairly refer to the Finance Committee to deal with, and he would move that that course be adopted. jiCotmcillor WILKINSON seconded. The Rev. JOHN YONGE thought it desirable for the Committee to have a little more information. So far as he could see the letter went in the right direction He andlthe members would like more information as 1 to the disparity which existed in the amounts likely to be received by the schools, because he hadheard rumours of a school receiving a very much larger amount of fees returned on the principle of one-fourth than it would appear !to beareasonable that its expenses would require, whereas one-fourth of a very much smaller sum given to other schools would not be a sufficiently large sum to meet their requirements. Mr. T. MAY thought it would meet the case if the Chairman's resolution were passed with a rider to the effect that the Finance Committee, instead of having this to deal with, should report to this committee. The CHAIR:MAN said that did not touch the point at all. There was no doubt that a principal was involved. Councillor WILKINSON : What about the free schools?

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY thought it would scarcely be desir- able to embark upon what would probably become a desultory discussion. He did not feel competent to go I into the details of the fees received by the various schools unless he had the figures before him, and if he had the figures he questioned whether it would be desirable to go into them in detail. There were one or two questions of principle rather than detail, and it was on the former that the Finance Committee arrived at their decision,which was confirmed by the Education Committee. The period for which the proposed per- centage of fees-was settled was, he thought,only for the first twelve months. It would be extremely undesirable to re-open the question until they had had that twelve months' experience as to how the system worked. The CHAIRMAN: I think that is an answer to your question Mr. Yonge. The Rev. Jolng YONGE : It is an answer, but I do not deem it a satisfactory one. (Laughter.) The CHAIRMAN: That may be. It all depends upon what point you judge it from. Councillor JOHN ROBERTS rose to speak, but the Chairman ruled him out of order, and remarked that there would be no further need to discuss the question as it would come up at the next meeting of the Educa- tion Committee on the minutes of the Finance Committee.

===== 0002601_19030926_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
LATCHFORD NATIONAL SCHOOL.

The DIRECTOR read the following letter from the Board of Education :—" With reference to Draft Final Order No. 162, I am directed to inform you that upon consideration of the observations which have been sub- mitted to the Board of Education by persons interested in the Latchford National School (Christ Church), the Board have thoughtfit to modify the provisions of. their proposed Order. The Order WS amended will provide that Foundation Managers shall consist of :—(a) The principal officiating minister of the ecclesiastical parish til or district in hid' the school is for the time being situated (ex-o io.) (b) One person being a member of the Church f England, to be appointed by co-opta- tion. (c) Twd*ersons being members of the Church of England, to be elected by subscribers to the funds of the whop!. If it is desired to make any further observations upon the proposed Order, they should be sent to this office, within fourteen days from the date of this letter."

The DIRECTOR said the difference in the Order was that a person was to be co-opted and not appointed by the principal officiating minister. From a public point of view he thought that was an advantage.

NON-PROVIDED SCHOOLS.

The Director said the Board of Education hadlissued final orders in respect of Latchford (St. James's), Heathside National, St. Ann's, and St. Peter's schools. FINANCE COMMITTEE. THE ATTENDANCE OF TEACHERS TO BE RECORDED.

MR. A. J. JOLLEY AND THE "BLACK SHEEP."

The Finance Committee recommended the appoint- ment of school keepers as reported in our last issue. The Committee had also decided that with a view to the proper checking of the teachers' salary sheets, it is imperative that returns should be made weekly by the head teachers of the elementary schools showing the attendance of all the members of their respective staffs. ,In moving the adoption of the minutes, Mr. A. J. JOLLEY pointed out that on July 20th the committee agreed that there should be a minimum payment of £1 a week to every school keeper where the school had accommodation for 300 children,and that keepers should be paid an increase of is. for each place over the first 300. If a school keeper was unable to satisfactorily discharge the whole of the duties then he had to pay for any assistance required out of his remuneration. With regard to the attendance of teachers a resolution was brought forward in May "that7the head teacher of each school record the absence of all teachers and the reason thereof on the monthly attendance returns." An amendment deleting this motion was passed. The com- mittee were unanimous that the present, resolution should be brought forward. They were told in May that it was to the interests of managers and head teachers to see that subordinates were thoroughly efficient and attended to their dutier,and therefore cases of neglect would, in the interests of the schools, be reported. Theoretically that was right, but practically he regretted to say, it hadnot worked out so. They had to legislate for schools in which there might be laxity. He did not wish to disparage teachers, but the com- mittee had to provide for "black sheep," and unhappily, there were "black sheep" in the teaching profession as well as in others. It was that that led to the present resolution. Mr. R. T. PAIRCLOUGn seconded.

Councillor JAMES EVANS asked the Chairman of the Finance Committee which sub-committee was respons- ible for the account referred to at the last Town Coun- cil meeting that was stated to be 100. per cent. above what it was under the old regime. Tf the Chairman could not reply he would move a resolution that the whole of the details of the account be furnished to the Education Committee, and so pass through the Press to the public. Councillor UPSON said he still considered the resolu- tion with regard to the attendance of teachers a mis- take. Mr. Jolley said they had to legislate for "black sheep" but it was hardly the thing to treat them al as "black sheep." Alderman SMETHURST : He did not refer to the whole lot.

Councillor UPSON : The resolution applies to the whole lot. That is my point. Continuing he again pointed out that the absence of teachers was hound by the code to be recorded in the log book, which was open for inspection by,the officials and members of the committee, and he did not see the necessity for a reso- lution like the one proposed especially in view of the fact that the committee rejected a similar recommenda- tion by a large majority. Mr. MA.ir said unless they had some record of sick leave and absence there was such a thing as malingery, and people who were feeble and sick might be paid at the same rate as those able to carry out their duties. It was grossly unfair that active and attentive teachers should be burdened with the ills of idle and feeble el- adj utors.

M. R. T. FAIRCLOU6II thought it only right they should have a proper system of checking the attendance of teachers if they were to be held responsible to the town that the teachers were being paid for the duties they were doing. With their system of paying accounts they couldnot check them until neasly a month after they were paid. Councillor. WmfassoN said it was no use beating about the bush. At the last Finance Committee meet- ing they had a wages list from a certain school in town and happened to know that a teacher had been away from school for some time—he could litot say it was on account of sickness—and his wages appeared in full. The Rev. JoHN YONG': sympathized with Councillor Upson's feelings in the matter. The Me mentioned by Comte'nor Wilkinson was one they deplored. but at the same time he tad not think it gave them sufficient basis

===== 0002601_19030926_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
STATEMENT BY ALDE4KAN MONKS.

Alderman MONKS proposed the confirmation of the minutes of the School Management Committee. He referred to the voluminous character of the minutes and said the Director of Education was frnding the committee plenty of work, and the sittings of the com- mittee sometimes extended over three or four hours. Referring to the appointments of teachers for the even- ing classes at the Technical school, he said the staff as a whole, including the teachers for the Secondary school, would not exceed in the aggregate the teachers they had had in previous years, and the expenses altogether would be somewhat less. He was not in a. position to give the exact figures because the condi- tions had changed this year as compared with previous years, but he thought it would run from £lOO to £2OO a year less than twelve months ago. With regard to the evening continuation schools, these were merely carry- ing on the work done in previous years. The new centre was at the Wycliffe British, which was started because the committee felt the centre ought to be in that neighbourhood so as to be convenient for the children living more immediately around it. An addi- tional cookery mistress for Fairfield School had been rendered necessary by the large number of applications which had been received from children for instruction in cookery. The committee felt it their duty to recom- mend the appointment, which would involve an ex- penditure of about £7O a year. If all the children who had applied attended regularly and passed the examin- ation satisfactorily at the end of the course it was estimated that they would earn about £1.30, so that they might reasonably hope not to be out of pocket, but perhaps make a little. The income would come from the Government in the form of a grant, being 55. for every 20 hours put in by the scholars. Respecting the appointment of Mr. Murray as secretary for the local examination for the Cambridge Universities, Alderman Monks said that the Rev. Morley Stevenson had hitherto carried out this work but he was anxious to be relieved of it. With regard to the scholarships there were $1 candidates,that in the opinion of the inspectors, qualified for the entrance examination for the Secondary School, and 50 for the eximinat;ou for the pupil teachers' scholarships. Out of the 50 there were 35 vacancies. These %would he appointed, and the other , , 15 had been offered theopoortunity of joining as ordin- , axy students and paying the fee like others who would be admitted in the ordinary way. 1 Mr. JoLLEY seconded the adoption of the minutes.

Mr. R. T. FAIRCLOUGIi suggested that in cases of appointments it should be stated whether a mistress was being replaced or whether it -was a fresh appoint- ment, as they did not know where they were.

The CH 1.1.61XN promised that that would be done in the future.

MR. PLINSTON AND THE STATIONERY CONTRACT.

Mr. PLINSTON, referring to the acceptance of the tender of Messrs. Philip, Son and Nephew, for the supply of school materials, asked whether any firms in Warrington sent in tenders, and whether there was such a great difference between the amounts that they were compelled to send the order outside the borough. He thought that unless there was a large margin they ought to endeavour to do something for those who were paying the education rate. (Hear. hear.) He also wished to ask whether the fair wage clause was inserted and whether it applied to this particular contract. ' Councillor DODDS asked how many applications there had been for places in the new Secondary School. Mr. May suggested that the committee should exer- cise leniency with regard to the first entrance examina- tion for the Secondary school seeing that the candidates had had no time for preparation. He thought those who had tried hard to get in should be admitted. Councillor UPSON, replying to Mr. Plinston, said that he and Councillor Craik were the two persons who re- commcmded the acceptance of the contract for station- eryand materials. They spent three hOurs in examining the samples submitted, the quality of paper being a great consideration. They were anxious to place the tender locally, but they could not in justice recommend that course as local men were hopelessly out of the runn- ing as regarded prices and quality of material. I Alderman MONKS replying to the other portion of Mr.. Plinston's question stated that tha fair wage clause was inserted,and they required an assurance complying with it.

Mr. PLINSTON : That is quite satisfactory. Replying to Councillor Dodds, Alderman MONKS said the entries- for the examination were 63 pupil teachers and 35 for the Secondary school proper. Out of those 81 were appointed for the Secondary School. _ _

Councillor DODDS asked if any hdbeen rejected be- cause they were wanting room for 1,300 scholars. Alderman MONKS said he did not know what that had to do with it.

Councillor DODDS, remarked that it had a great deal to do with it. They had got all these teachers. Alderman MONKS said Councillor Dodds forgot alto- gether that they had to give instruction to the ppil teachers. Of the 81 who had been appointed for the Secondary school 17 pupil teachers had qualified by pass- ing the King's Scholarship examination and Cambridge Local Examinations. There were 35 pupil teachers' scholarships and 15 of the others had been allowed to join as ordinary students. As to Mr. May's question the Committee had decided upon cer- tain conditions. It was not for them to re-open the question.

The CHAIRMAN remarked that the Secondary school would be formally opened on Wednesday week at nine o'clock, and he would be glad if members would be pre- sent.

The minutes were approved. _ _"BOYS ON THE CLIO."

Councillor ELLISON moved the confirmation of the minutes of the School Attendance Committee, and briefly alluded to the report of Mr. Murray on the Warrington boys on the Shio"Clio,"which was printed in last Saturday's 'Examiner."

The CHAIRMAN said the suggestion in the report that the lads should be kept from their old haunts was a good one. He hoped some steps would .ba taken to carry the suggestion out. (Hear, hear). The meeting then terminated.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football. By " Ovalist." Warrington's form was evidently too good to last. After clever victories over Leigh and Hull, they visited the heights of Batley last Saturday, and found the "gallant youths" too smart for them. Last season Warrington won both engagements with the Batley men, but the latter are still full of fight and they were delighted to once more make their old foes from War- rington bow the knee. We have still a good deal to wipe off the Batley account, and until we have the extreme felicity to knock them out of the cup-ties the account will hardly be made to balance.

To say the least, Warrington went away with high hopes and plenty of confidence in themselves, but they returned in a humbler frame of mind. Batley had lost both their previous engagements and were there- fore exceedingly keen on gaining their first points. They beat Warrington in the same sweet way as of yore, by keeping the ball close, and playing on the visiting third line for all they were worth. The home forwards showed some of the form which made them an octet to be feared not very long ago, and they out-generalled the Warrington lot. The margin of nine points was, however, more than the homesters were entitled to on the day's play.

On the whole, the display of the Warrington men was very disappointing, both forward and back. Edmunds was ill before the game, and soon after the start he received an injury to the shoulder which put him out of action entirely. This, no doubt, tended to disorganize the Warrington men, but there was little excuse for the fumbling of first one man and then another when they got the ball. Combination was conspicuously absent, and a better display will be looked for against Keighley to-day.

Salford accomplished the great feat of the after- noon by defeating Broughton Rangers at Broughton by no less than 20 points to 6 points. The Reds appear to have a very formidable side once more. Will they win any honours I wonder. After last season's catastrophe one almost despairs of them do- ing their club justice in the final contests for supre- macy. Oldham also made two more points at the expense of Huddersfield, while Kingston Rovers notched their third win against Bradford. Hunslet gained a somewhat fortunate victory against Run- corn, and are thus bracketed with the other three un- defeated clubs.

A curious feature of the league results so far, is that Broughton Rangers have had more points scored 'against them than any other 'club.

Pedestrianism.

The authorities of the National Cyclists Union (Manchester centre) have informed H. Coppack, who took part in a shop assistants' walking contest at Winsford, that he must apply for reinstatement as an amateur, as he suspended himself by competing in an unregistered walk. He is not .required to return the. prizes he has won since the competition in question Coppack is well known in local athletic circles, and figured at Wilderspool in the cycle races on the occa- sion of the police sports:

The Warrington Works League rapidly takes shape. A. number of rules have been drawn up and a meet- ing of the, League will be held in the W.F.C. Pavilion on Tuesday -evening next. Six clubs have already joined, viz. : Crostield's; Rylands', L. and N.-W., St. Helens Cable Company, Naylor's, and Walker's. The annual subscription for each club is 2s. 6d. In order to facilitate. the formation of further clubs, it has been decided that two or mire works may com- bine, subject t 9 the approval of the League Committee. Where a greund is not available, the Latchford Rangers ground will be utilised.

Angling. The Latchford Angling Society fished their last. match of the season on Saturday at Northwich. The party drove to Northwich in a four-in-hand, and were favoured with fine weather. Unfortunately the sport was very poor. There were 24 competitors for the 16 prizes offered, but only seven caught fish, the weights ranging from a little over 4ozs. to loz. 2drms. THE PROPOSED DIVERSION OF THE RIVER.

A meeting of local traders and representatives of the Corporation was held at the Town Hall during the past week to take into consideration, the important scheme submitted for their approval by the directors of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Full details of the scheme were published in the " Examiner " lash week. Briefly stated, the Canal Company pro- pose to effect , a diversion of the river, commencing at a point nearly opposite the Cable works and cutting across the intervening land to a point just below Messrs. Monks Hall and Company's works. A lock and sluices will be con' structed about half-way between the two points mentioned. The bend of the river which would thus be diverted will be impounded and made practically into a dock. The level of the water in the river round Warrington is to be maintained at lift. Gun- above ordnance datum. The Corporation is asked to pledge its credit for the whole cost of the works, the interest and sinking fund to be repaid by the Canal Company in yearly instalments until the loan is liquidated. It is further proposed that the Corporation shall bear the cost of the diversion of sewers which may be necessary. The matter has been referred to Sir John Wolfe Barry to advise the Corporation thereovi, and whew his report is received a joint meeting of the traders and the Rivers Committee will he held.

THE MAYOR'S GIFT TO THE TOWN. A Bust of the King.

It will be remembered that last year the Mayor (rVII-. J. C. Parr) decided to present a bust of the King to the' town as a Coronation gift. The work was entrusted to Mr. Drury, and he has executed his commission-' a manner which, we believe, will give entire satisfac- tion. The bust is to be placed in the vestibule of the, Town Hall next week and preparations are already in Progress with this end in viewit is proposed to remover the graceful figure'representing"History"by I. T. Moore sculptor, from the niche which it at present occupies and place it on a pedestal near the fire place on the opposite side of the vestibule. The bust of the Kin will be placed in the niche when the removal has taken place.

BOWLING. The Warrington Works Handicap The Warrington Works Individual Merit. Handicap commenced a few weeks ago will be concluded this (Saturday) afternoon on the Green attached to the • King and Queen" Hotel. Afterwards the prizes will be distributed together with the awards gained in the recent works team competition. HANDICAP AT BANK QUAY. On Saturday afternoon a handicap arranged for the benefit of Mr. Smith, Bowling Green terrace, who has been incapacitated from following his employment, was commenced on the green adjoining the "Bowling Green" Inn. 228 entries have been received and it is expected Ithat the first round will be concluded this afternoon and the second round commenced. THE WARRINGTON BUILDING • SOCIETY SENSATION. - We understand that the action .for damages which has. been instituted by the directors of the Warrington Permanent Benefit Building Society against three of ,their former officials will be heard in the Nisi Prius Court at Liverpool in November next. The directors are claiming heavy damages from Mr. Robert Proud, the late secretary, and Messrs. James Bolton and W. Dick, formerly auditors.

CHESHIRE EDUCATION COMMITTEE. _ Nonconformists and Religious Instruction. A meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee tools place at Crewe on Monday, under the presidency of Mr. C. E. Thosnyoroft. On the motion of Dr. Hewitt, seconded by the Rev. J. Grant Bird, it was decided to instruct the Director of Education to obtain information in regard to the day training colleges in connection with the Universities at Liverpool and Manchester, and the probable cost of providing hostels; and also to obtain information as to the cost likely to be incurred in providing a separate training college for teachers in Cheshire. A long dis- cussion then took place as to the withdrawal of the children of Nonconformist parents from religious instruction in schools where such a desire was made known. Dr. Hodgson moved that the third bylaw, dealing with the withdrawal of children from (instruction in religious subjects where the parent wished it, be opera- tive-an every district of the county. Mr. Smale seconded, and on a division 12 voted for putting the bylaw into operation at once, and 12 in favour of deferring further consideration of the ques- tion. The Chairman gave his casting vote in favour of postponing the subject.
